## REC-022: locally resolved numeric proxy tunnels (partial follow-up)

The preceding fail-closed proxy restriction now has a compatible path for locally resolvable hostname targets. When enabled task/global IP blocks require a concrete target, native custom HTTP resolves once, chooses the first approved IP, and uses that numeric destination through the configured proxy. HTTP/HTTPS proxies use CONNECT even for HTTP origins: an ordinary absolute-form request cannot reliably preserve the original Host independently of its numeric request URI. SOCKS5/SOCKS5h receive numeric addresses using Go's existing proxy protocol implementation. Host, encoded path/query, POST method/body and origin TLS hostname retain their logical identity. HTTPS proxy TLS verifies the proxy separately; Basic proxy credentials remain on CONNECT and do not enter origin headers. URL/IP policy is reloaded after lookup, before proxy dialing, and again before HTTP CONNECT after the proxy handshake/header callback. A failed lookup, blocked result or failed tunnel does not use remote DNS, direct fallback, or replay application bytes to another candidate. Only the first approved proxy candidate is attempted; direct dual-stack fallback is unchanged.

Inspection of third_party/go-mitmproxy/proxy/attacker.go shows ServeHTTP reconstructs URL.Host from req.Host after interception. Therefore the configured recording endpoint is explicitly refused on this hostname/IP-restricted path before local DNS or proxy requests; the numeric tunnel change does not claim recording-path target confinement or preserved capture metadata. Unrestricted hostname recording and checked literal-IP admission retain their earlier behavior. External proxy peer attestation, proxy-only DNS, additional-candidate fallback, recording integration, and arbitrary shell/Python/MCP runtime enforcement remain open. REC-022 remains partial. The native recording route also had a separate URL bug: Manager.ProxyAddr already returns a complete URL, so httpProxyTransport now uses it without prepending a second http://.

Verification: GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./server -run '^Test(HTTP|Pinned|RecordingHTTP|WorkerHTTP|RunHTTPTool|CustomHTTP|CustomConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule|CustomOperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with the existing Python interpreter, explicit disposable transport PostgreSQL/config and SKOPOS_DESTINATION_E2E=1 passed 22 top-level Windows tests, zero failures/skips (pinned-proxy-windows-tests.log, 9.078s). This includes real task/global IP rule checks, 36 worker destination cases, actual resolved-operation SDK fixtures, direct DNS/TLS/fallback regressions, preserved HTTP POST/path/Host, new block refusal, separate origin/proxy TLS verification, recording endpoint refusal and pre-CONNECT policy changes. A subsequent test-only expansion adds Basic proxy credential assertions; its focused Windows TLS test passed (pinned-proxy-auth-windows-tests.log, 1.564s). The rebuilt Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 server binary passed 21 top-level tests, zero failures/skips, including those credential assertions and both native SOCKS5/SOCKS5h numeric handshakes (pinned-proxy-linux-tests.log). The Linux selection omits the Python SDK matrix because that container has no Python. Fixtures contact only loopback and synthetic documentation/nonresolving destinations; the PostgreSQL matrix restores prior global rules and removes created task rules. Logs and binaries are under LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS. Full server/frontend and real remote/MITM proxy compatibility were not run.

Windows go test ./locale -count=1 -v also passed all 11 tests (pinned-proxy-locale-tests.log). Edited-file gofmt and git diff --check passed. Checkpoint remains 34 fixed/five partial families. No paid scanner/provider, download/install, deployment, commit/push or OS change occurred.

## REC-022: proxy-side DNS cannot silently bypass IP restrictions (partial follow-up)

Native custom HTTP previously skipped target-IP checks whenever a proxy was selected, preserving remote DNS but also bypassing task/global IP rules for hostname destinations. The new AssetStore.CheckHTTPProxyDestination reloads global/task block rules and refuses proxy-side hostname resolution when any enabled exact_ip, fuzzy_ip or cidr block needs a concrete target peer. Literal-IP proxy targets use existing CheckHTTPPeer; a proxy infrastructure IP is never treated as the origin. With no enabled IP blocks, existing hostname proxy routing remains available without substituting unrelated local DNS results. The transport calls this policy before proxy RoundTrip for every initial request and redirect, after operation review and ordinary actual-URL authorization. Database-load failures refuse execution. Errors are bilingual and omit URLs/credentials/query tokens.

This is an interim admission restriction with a material compatibility limit: legitimate hostname proxy workflows under IP restrictions now need direct routing or an allowed literal-IP destination. A verified proxy peer path is still needed to preserve those hostname workflows while enforcing restrictions; this change does not assert that capability, inspect the remote proxy's actual peer, or replace the project's wider proxy requirements. Ordinary unrestricted hostname routing and literal-IP proxy routing remain supported. External/manual recording-proxy clients, shell/Python/MCP and arbitrary runtimes remain separate scope boundaries. REC-022 remains partial rather than treating the fail-closed restriction as complete workflow-compatible runtime confinement.

Tests use a real loopback HTTP proxy fixture, synthetic nonresolving hostname/documentation-range IP destinations and explicit disposable transport PostgreSQL. The matrix covers task/global exact-IP, fuzzy-IP and CIDR blocks, allowed literal IPs, denied literal IPs, hostname retry after removal, missing policy database and URL-token redaction. Prior enabled global IP rules in the disposable fixture are temporarily isolated and restored; created rules/tasks are cleaned up. A transport-level case checks that no blocked request reaches the proxy and that the unrestricted retry still routes through it without checking proxy infrastructure or doing local target DNS. A diagnostic overlay removes only the proxy policy invocation: the matrix observes a successful proxy response where policy should block (proxy-dns-scope-before-tests.log, expected go test exit 1). The checkout is not modified by the overlay.

Verification: Windows GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./server -run '^Test(HTTP|WorkerHTTP|RunHTTPTool|CustomHTTP|CustomConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule|CustomOperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit existing Python/disposable transport PostgreSQL/configuration and SKOPOS_DESTINATION_E2E=1 passed 16 top-level tests, zero failures/skips (proxy-dns-scope-windows-tests.log, 9.060s). Current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 native server binary passed the same selection excluding the Python SDK matrix: 15 tests, zero failures/skips (proxy-dns-scope-linux-tests.log). Both include 36 real worker destination-policy subcases and direct DNS/TLS/dual-stack regressions. ## REC-034: deferred trap actions and alias definitions are reviewed (partial follow-up)

Trap actions and alias values were previously treated as builtin argument data, so quoted destructive commands inside them were not projected into canonical policy subjects. deferredShellPrograms now returns literal trap actions and alias replacement text to the existing bounded nested-program parser, and marks generated/unsupported forms as unresolved programs. Supported builtin wrappers now include trap and alias. Literal restricted actions are denied by existing rules before the uncertainty fallback. Even a literal alias definition requires real human approval if no earlier rule decides it: replacement fragments can combine with later argv and session state, so inspecting the fragment alone does not prove the future operation safe. Alias -p alongside definitions still inspects those definitions. Explicit operator allow priorities and disabled interception remain unchanged.

Known trap listing (-p/-P/-l, including combinations), reset/empty actions and single literal signal-reset forms remain nonexecuting data. Literal alias queries/listing remain data. Listing output later executed by an opaque shell still encounters the existing stdin/program uncertainty gate. No handlers run and no alias expansion/session-state mutation occurs during analysis. Existing function bodies are already traversed by the AST; an explicit regression confirms literal restricted function-body operations remain denied. Unsupported aliases inherited from earlier sessions, interpreter-specific semantics, startup files and runtime/OS containment remain outside this change.

Initial focused regressions exposed builtin trap not being unwrapped; the wrapper whitelist was extended to actual trap/alias builtins, preserving non-builtin data handling. All focused regressions then passed. A diagnostic source overlay removes only deferred-program integration from operation_subject.go, leaving current wrappers and unrelated controls intact: the literal trap regression fails because the quoted deferred operation receives no policy decision (deferred-shell-before-tests.log, expected go test exit 1). The checkout is not modified by the overlay.

Native Linux Bash fixtures confirm both deferred execution paths with an inert locally defined rm function that writes only a private marker. They execute trap EXIT and alias substitution through eval, with empty inherited shell environment except the explicit marker/PATH and --noprofile/--norc; no real rm executable or destructive operation runs. Source: [Bash trap semantics](https://www.gnu.org/software/bash/manual/html_node/Bourne-Shell-Builtins.html), [alias substitution](https://www.gnu.org/software/bash/manual/html_node/Aliases.html). Native installed Bash help was also inspected. These describe shell behavior, not sandbox enforcement.

Verification: GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit disposable PostgreSQL passed 61 top-level Windows tests (47 intercept, three guard, 11 locale), zero failures/skips (deferred-shell-windows-tests.log; guard 28.139s). Current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 native binaries passed 47 intercept and three guard tests, zero failures/skips (deferred-shell-intercept-linux-tests.log, deferred-shell-guard-linux-tests.log), including the two native Bash trap/alias subcases. Real pending-decision/audit guard coverage increased to 360 subcases on each platform, exercising generated handlers, malformed handlers, alias fragments and generated definitions with human allow/deny/no-decision, English/Korean and three execution tool identities. Dispatcher stubs send no target traffic. Windows SDK compatibility passed both tests without failures/skips: go test ./server -run '^TestCustom(ConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule|OperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK)$' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit existing Python/disposable PostgreSQL/configuration (deferred-shell-sdk-tests.log, 5.557s). Logs/binaries/diagnostic overlays are under LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS. Edited-file gofmt and diff whitespace checks passed. ## REC-004: CLI-generated methods and sensitive long-option prefixes (partial follow-up)

Disabling startup configuration did not make every literal command statically predictable: curl can expand its own variables in --expand-request and --expand-config. These are not shell expansions and previously passed literal-argument analysis. New unresolvedMethodOption identifies expanded method/config selectors and prefixes of sensitive long options (curl request/config/expanded selectors, wget method/config/execute). They enter the existing real-human approval gate when restrictive policy is active and no earlier rule decides the operation. Exact ordinary --request/--method retain static denial; header/body option values and --request-target retain data/path semantics. Prefixes are conservatively reviewed without assuming a particular installed CLI version accepts them. Explicit operator allows, disabled interception and timeout/cancellation denial remain unchanged.

The System32 curl.exe native regression now proves six private-configuration/loopback cases, including DELETE produced by variable-expanded request and variable-expanded configuration even with first -q. Installed curl reports version 8.21.0; children use CREATE_NO_WINDOW. A diagnostic source overlay removes only the new unresolvedMethodOption admission call: the native regression fails because the expanded request receives no policy decision (expanded-http-method-before-tests.log, expected go test exit 1). The overlay preserves previous startup/configuration gates and does not modify the checkout. With the admission call, policy checks require approval and the deliberately executed inert local fixture confirms the effective DELETE method. This is not a target attack or OS-level enforcement experiment.

Primary semantics: [curl variables](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html) documents variable expansion in option parameters; [GNU getopt_long](https://www.sourceware.org/glibc/manual/2.43/html_node/Getopt-Long-Options.html) documents long-option abbreviations. CLI-generated curl methods/configuration are natively reproduced. Prefix reviews, including GNU wget forms, have semantic policy and real guard-approval coverage, not native GNU wget reproduction or proof that all installed curl versions accept abbreviations.

Verification: GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit disposable PostgreSQL passed 58 top-level Windows tests (44 intercept, three guard, 11 locale), zero failures/skips (expanded-http-method-windows-tests.log; guard 22.794s). Current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 native binaries passed all 43 intercept and three guard tests, zero failures/skips (expanded-http-method-intercept-linux-tests.log and expanded-http-method-guard-linux-tests.log). Real guard/pending-decision/audit coverage increased to 288 subcases on each platform, including CLI-generated curl methods and abbreviated wget methods, with human allow/deny/no-decision, English/Korean, and three execution tool identities. Dispatcher stubs send no target traffic. Windows SDK compatibility passed both tests without failures/skips: go test ./server -run '^TestCustom(ConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule|OperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK)$' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit existing Python/disposable PostgreSQL/configuration (expanded-http-method-sdk-tests.log, 5.651s). Diagnostic overlay/script and all logs are under LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS. Edited-file gofmt and diff whitespace checks passed. Full server/frontend, native GNU wget, all CLI option grammars and runtime/OS/network method containment were not run or established.

## REC-004: implicit HTTP startup configuration requires human approval (partial follow-up)

Curl/wget command analysis now treats default startup files as unresolved method inputs. A mutable local rc file is not read or attested at preflight. When a restrictive input policy is active and no earlier explicit rule decides the operation, the existing unresolved-method path requires a real human decision, disables model fallback and denies after the five-minute timeout/cancellation. Curl must explicitly disable default config with literal -q or --disable as its first argument to avoid this new uncertainty; wget must supply a genuine --no-config option before --. Late curl -q, wget --no-config used as option data or after --, and explicit config even alongside a disabling flag remain reviewable. Explicit/runtime request arguments retain their existing checks. Disabled interception and explicit operator allow-rule priority remain unchanged.

Canonical HTTP program identity also recognizes curl.exe/wget.exe, ASCII case and explicit Windows paths for startup uncertainty and static DELETE method denial. This is lexical program identification, not executable attestation; aliases, replacement binaries and unsupported executors still require separate containment. Safe option-data tests now explicitly disable startup files: their original premise that non-method argv implies a predictable method was incomplete. Original default-config commands remain covered as approval-required cases. A new wget -- data fixture originally omitted the hostname used by its explicit-allow test; the fixture was corrected to retain that hostname. Initial failed assumptions output is retained in default-http-config-test-assumptions-before.log; no assertions were weakened or known rejection changed to success.

A native Windows test uses the installed System32 curl.exe, a private CURL_HOME/.curlrc and a synthetic loopback handler. Default configuration changes a plain request to DELETE; late -q still sends DELETE, first -q sends GET, and explicit -K still supplies DELETE even with first -q. Matching checks require approval for the unresolved cases. This reproduction deliberately sends DELETE only to the inert local fixture, not through an unapproved real target. Native child launches use CREATE_NO_WINDOW. User rc files, credentials, external targets, installations and providers are not used. GNU wget startup behavior is supported by its official manual and semantic policy tests; no native GNU wget reproduction was run.

Primary semantics: [curl manual, --disable](https://curl.se/docs/manpage.html#-q) requires first-argument disabling of default configuration; [GNU wget manual](https://www.gnu.org/software/wget/manual/wget.html) documents startup files, --no-config and explicit --config taking priority. These sources support CLI behavior, not OS/network enforcement.

Verification: GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit disposable PostgreSQL passed 58 top-level Windows tests (44 intercept, three guard, 11 locale), zero failures/skips; logs default-http-config-windows-tests.log (guard 19.988s). Current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 native binaries passed all 43 intercept and three guard tests, zero failures/skips (default-http-config-intercept-linux-tests.log and default-http-config-guard-linux-tests.log). The real guard/pending-decision/audit matrix passes 252 subcases on each platform, including implicit curl/wget configuration and explicit wget override. Dispatcher stubs do not send target traffic; native curl reproduction is separate. Focused Windows SDK tests passed two top-level tests with no failures/skips: go test ./server -run '^TestCustom(ConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule|OperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK)$' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit existing Python/disposable PostgreSQL/configuration (default-http-config-sdk-tests.log, 5.416s). ## REC-024: capture-off enforced at recorder persistence (partial follow-up)

Previously the manager toggle only removed proxy/tool/prompt routing from rebuilt agents. The live sink still called record for clients directly addressing the proxy, including existing connections. Traffic now exposes SetCaptureEnabled and CaptureEnabled; the recorder checks its capture-off flag under the same write lock that covers body/index persistence. Disabling acquires that lock and waits for an already-admitted write to finish, establishing the guarantee that no later record can commit while disabled. Intentional off returns before ID generation, quota queries or body/index allocation and does not count as a dropped capture. Existing read/export/delete and archive restoration are unchanged. Standalone traffic.Open retains its prior default of recording; manager-owned traffic takes the persisted default-off setting.

Manager startup loads capture state before opening/starting the listener and applies it to the recorder before launching Start. SetTrafficEnabled serializes persistence and recorder/manager state under the manager mutex, applies the write gate before publishing the new manager flag, then performs existing browser MCP routing synchronization outside that lock. Proxy client admission, upstream TLS validation and forwarding remain active with capture off. Completion-time state governs in-flight responses: a response completed while off is skipped; if recording is re-enabled before completion it may be captured. This is persistence control, not a no-MITM or network body-buffer guarantee.

Verification: native Windows GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s passed 113 top-level tests (102 traffic, 11 locale), zero failures and one existing opt-in transport skip (capture-toggle-windows-tests.log, traffic 24.378s). Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic passed 101, zero failures and the same skip (capture-toggle-linux-tests.log). Windows server command go test ./server -run '^Test(TrafficCaptureToggle|TaskArchive|Archive|FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|TrafficStorage)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/configuration passed 22, no failures/skips (capture-toggle-server-tests.log, 8.578s). The manager regression verifies persisted-off startup, repeated enabling/disabling and matching database/manager/recorder state. Native traffic tests verify disabling waits for the existing write lock, prevents later captures and preserves callback evidence workflows.

The direct-proxy regression was then expanded to HTTP and HTTPS subcases without further production edits. Focused Windows and Linux runs both passed two top-level tests and both protocol subcases (capture-toggle-http-https-windows-tests.log and capture-toggle-http-https-linux-tests.log). Each uses real local target/proxy sockets and checks response bytes, initial no-body/no-row allocation, on/off recording counts, unchanged saved evidence, export availability and no dropped-quota increment. HTTPS clients validate the proxy CA; the local self-signed target uses the supported explicit exact-origin TLS exception. No provider or external target is used. All logs are under LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS. Edited-file gofmt and diff whitespace checks passed. Full server/frontend, race-detector, active write-transaction stress and unrelated protocol runtime checks were not run.

REC-024 remains partial for bounded retention, growth-time reserve enforcement and retained/metadata/legacy/aggregate storage. Checkpoint remains 34 fixed/five partial. No paid scan/provider, dependency download, deployment, commit/push or global OS change occurred.

## REC-024: per-exchange and per-host capture limits (partial follow-up)

New recorder writes check configurable positive SKOPOS_TRAFFIC_EXCHANGE_BYTES (default 64 MiB) and SKOPOS_TRAFFIC_HOST_BYTES (default 256 MiB) before spilling either body or opening an index transaction. Logical accounting includes full request/response lengths, stored headers, URL/host/method/template/content-type metadata and 128 bytes per row. Duplicate bodies count for each retained exchange, while separate blob/index/WAL budgets govern physical allocation. Hostname accounting groups ports, ASCII case and trailing dots; it does not unify every DNS alias, IDN representation or equivalent IPv6 spelling. Requests to different textual hosts still encounter the global storage budgets. Rejected captures report a drop and preserve callback response bytes and previously committed evidence.

Retained rows determine host usage under the existing write lock. Restart, deletion and archive restore therefore share accounting rather than resetting an in-memory counter. Explicit archive restoration deliberately retains its existing physical storage budgets and can restore evidence above capture limits; subsequent captures for that host are refused. Existing over-limit evidence remains readable/exportable/deletable. These limits apply to recording after proxy buffering, not network body buffering, automatic eviction or a filesystem-wide quota. Host accounting scans retained metadata and can add latency for large collections. An experimental expression index caused the minimum 64-KiB WAL committed-purge-with-active-reader regression to fail with SQLITE_BUSY on Windows/Linux; that index was removed, preserving the existing acceptance test and allowance. No test assertion or capacity was weakened.

The restart fixture exposed a pre-existing collision: second-resolution exchange IDs with a reset/modulo sequence could replace existing evidence. New IDs retain the timestamp/sequence prefix and add a cryptographic 128-bit nonce; recorder SQL uses INSERT rather than replacement, so any collision rejects the new capture. Existing opaque IDs remain valid. The restart test observes retained count after immediate close/reopen and fresh capture.

Logs: capture-budget-windows-tests.log and capture-budget-linux-tests.log under LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS. Tests cover pre-spill body rejection, oversized headers, callback response preservation, unchanged/exportable saved evidence, shared-blob logical charging, host case/dot/port variants, separate hosts, restart, explicit deletion, restored-evidence accounting and invalid configuration. Focused server archive/restore/finding/cleanup/storage tests passed 21 without failures/skips (capture-budget-server-tests.log, 6.515s). ## REC-024: native available-space admission margin and source-scope audit

The original REC-024 record explicitly calls for aggregate capture budgets, per-exchange/per-host limits, bounded retention, reserved free space and defined capture-off behavior. Previously implemented blob/index/WAL/scratch and archive bounds address several allocation paths but do not by themselves satisfy all of those requirements. Retained archive lifetime limits and staging metadata are additional hardening; they must not replace the original outstanding capture requirements.

Traffic.Open now parses SKOPOS_TRAFFIC_FREE_BYTES (positive bytes, default 256 MiB) without refusing to open existing evidence when that margin exceeds available space. New capture/index-import admission reads native available volume bytes for the runtime account: Windows GetDiskFreeSpaceEx on the actual data directory, or Unix Statfs.Bavail with overflow-safe conversion. Errors stop write admission. Unsupported platform builds report unavailable accounting instead of silently permitting new writes. Newly allocated capture/import blobs also check space before publication, including a metadata allowance for empty new blobs. Capture/import recheck index headroom after body copies; existing rollback paths remove newly created blobs if that later check fails. Read/export, idempotent blob reuse and explicit deletion/reclamation remain available.

Native Windows/Linux regressions configure a reserve larger than real available space rather than filling the user's disk. Capture drops before new body allocation, the flow's response bytes and existing rows stay unchanged, saved traffic still exports/deletes, and import rejects without allocating body files. Reopening with a usable margin permits retry. Native missing-path queries and invalid/overflowed configuration fail. This is free-space admission, not an exclusive OS reservation or a guarantee against concurrent external writers, filesystem metadata, sparse allocation or SQLite growth exceeding its estimate. Native growth-time enforcement, per-host/per-exchange quotas and capture-off semantics still need work.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed 107 top-level Windows tests (96 traffic, 11 locale), zero failures and one existing transport opt-in skip (`free-reserve-full-windows-tests.log`, traffic 22.370s). A current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic binary passed 95 tests, zero failures and the same skip (`free-reserve-full-linux-tests.log`). Windows `go test ./server -run '^Test(TaskArchive|Archive|FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|TrafficStorage)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/configuration passed 21 tests without failures/skips (`free-reserve-server-tests.log`, 6.793s). Focused native tests passed (`free-reserve-first-tests.log`). .env.example/Compose and bilingual README were updated; Compose configuration validation and diff whitespace checks passed. Full server/frontend, real low-disk stress, physical reservation, race-detector and other-platform runtime tests were not run.

## REC-024: shared archive payload staging and atomic evidence-copy retries (previous checkpoint)

Each archive job now counts regular files moved into its payload without following symlinks, then shares the remaining SKOPOS_TASK_ARCHIVE_BYTES allowance across streamed database history, traffic manifest/body exports and evidence copies. The shared writer checks capacity before every delegated write and serializes accounting. Final snapshot-manifest encoding is limited to capacity remaining after the rest of the payload; packaging still independently caps raw totals and compressed output. Existing ordinary traffic/evidence export APIs retain their behavior; writer-aware archive variants apply the job's policy to every generated/copy stream. Moved files add no copy allocation but consume the logical payload allowance. Compressed output remains separately capped, so this is not a single bound for payload plus package or all retained archives.

Evidence copies previously created the final hash-named destination before io.Copy; a denied/failed copy could leave a partial file that later retries rejected as corrupt. Copies now use private temporary files, sync/close before exclusive publication through the existing platform helper, preserve verified existing copies, and remove failed temporary output. The direct-publication diagnostic overlay reproduces retry failure on a partial hash-named body (`archive-stage-evidence-direct-tests.log`); the current native regression retries successfully in the same destination. Another fixture with 32 KiB already consumed from a 64-KiB stage rejects oversized traffic/evidence export writes, publishes no failed traffic manifest, preserves hot traffic and source evidence, and never exceeds the shared allowance. Existing worker quota failure/requeue/archive/restore still preserves upload bytes and findings.

## REC-024: archive package/extraction and streamed staging byte limits (previous checkpoint)

Task archive byte capacity now defaults to 4 GiB through SKOPOS_TASK_ARCHIVE_BYTES, validated as positive and at most the existing 128-TiB corruption ceiling. Packaging bounds the serialized snapshot manifest, cumulative raw file payload and compressed package output before writes/publication. Extraction checks compressed input size before checksum/decompression and checks cumulative expanded entry sizes with subtraction before creating an over-limit entry, avoiding overflow in the former total+header.Size expression. Streamed database/LLM history passes through a bounded writer before entering its staging file. Existing archival permissions, hashing, tar/zstd format, journal rollback and restore retry remain in place. .env.example, Compose and bilingual README now describe/pass through the allowance.

Native regressions prove oversized compressed expansion is rejected before writing its entry and preserves the original package, larger configured capacity restores it, a failed package leaves no published/partial package and rolls staged hot files back byte-for-byte, and rejected stream writes do not grow the staging file. Invalid/overflowed capacities fail validation. A real disposable PostgreSQL archive-worker test proves quota failure preserves the hot task finding and upload; raising capacity permits requeue/archive and full restore with identical upload bytes. Existing package round-trip, symlink policy, traversal, staging/restore/deletion journals and finding/evidence exports still pass.

The diagnostic overlay returning the legacy 128-TiB ceiling instead of the configured allowance reproduces oversized expansion/package acceptance (`archive-budget-unconfigured-reproduction-tests.log`). It keeps current security changes intact and is a capacity-bypass diagnostic, not a replay of an untouched source revision. Its initial overlay compile failure is retained separately in `archive-budget-unconfigured-tests.log` and is not security evidence.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./server ./locale -run '^Test(TaskArchive|Archive|FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|ProductionCatalog)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/configuration passed 20 top-level Windows server tests and one locale integrity test, zero failures/skips (`archive-budget-final-windows-tests.log`, server 6.212s). The current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 server binary with the equivalent filter/internal disposable PostgreSQL passed 23 tests, zero failures/skips (`archive-budget-final-linux-tests.log`). Focused worker and package checks also passed (`archive-budget-worker-tests.log`, `archive-budget-initial-tests.log`). `docker compose --env-file .env.example config --quiet` and `git -c core.safecrlf=false diff --check` passed. ## REC-024: shared SQLite scratch budget across concurrent connections (previous checkpoint)

Temporary databases/transient databases, sorter spill journals and subjournals now share one live scratch allowance per traffic VFS, initialized before connections open. It equals the existing derived temporary-file ceiling. Native writes, truncate growth and allocation hints reserve capacity under the same VFS mutex before delegating allocation; reservations also include an opened file's existing size. Successful truncation and native close release capacity. Failed native writes retain their reservation because a partial write may already have allocated bytes. Allocation hints reconcile against the confirmed native file size instead of retaining unused hinted capacity. Callback output storage uses the pinned libc TLS allocator rather than a movable Go stack address.

The main rollback journal retains a separate per-file ceiling so read/sort scratch cannot consume space required for atomic deletion/recovery. This is aggregate scratch accounting for live native SQLite files, not a filesystem-wide quota across all traffic artifacts; retained/failed-delete files, archive staging, legacy data and aggregate free-disk handling remain open.

The diagnostic overlay disabling only scratch accounting reproduces successful allocation by a second pooled connection beyond the shared allowance (`scratch-vfs-before-boundary-tests.log`), while per-file caps stay enabled. The native regression proves one 40-KiB capture can be copied into a temporary table, a competing table is rejected even though it fits the 256-KiB per-file cap, the first table remains intact, hints/truncate/sparse-write growth cannot bypass the shared 64-KiB budget, and native connection close releases capacity for a subsequent successful copy. Four independently checked-out connections also attempt allocation simultaneously; actual native file sizes and reservations stay within the shared ceiling, and the committed capture remains readable. These are native SQLite operations with fixture-only lowered allowances, not mock storage callbacks.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic ./locale -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed 104 top-level native Windows tests (93 traffic, 11 locale), zero failures and one existing transport opt-in skip (`scratch-vfs-full-windows-tests.log`, traffic 22.858s). A current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic binary passed 92 tests, zero failures and the same skip (`scratch-vfs-full-linux-tests.log`). Focused quota/concurrency tests passed in `scratch-vfs-shared-tests.log`/`scratch-vfs-parallel-tests.log`. Bilingual README behavior was updated. Full server/frontend, race-detector, other platforms and non-loopback opt-in tests are not covered by these results.

Windows `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./server -run '^Test(FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|TrafficStorage)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/configuration also passed six focused integration tests, zero failures/skips (`scratch-vfs-server-tests.log`, 5.178s), including evidence/API export, archive retry and PostgreSQL deletion rollback restoring staged files/traffic. `git -c core.safecrlf=false diff --check` passed.

## REC-024: temporary-file allocation and failed WAL restoration (previous checkpoint)

The store-specific VFS now applies its bounded native writes/truncate/allocation-control callbacks to SQLITE_OPEN_TEMP_DB and SQLITE_OPEN_TRANSIENT_DB as well as journal files; super-journals also use the journal ceiling. Traffic.Open initializes the temporary per-file allowance before any connection opens, using the same derived ceiling as rollback journals (twice the larger of configured index capacity and startup main-file size, plus 1 MiB). SQLite can sort/compact normally within this allowance; an oversized operation returns SQLITE_FULL while existing committed traffic remains readable. This is a per-file cap, not an aggregate disk or memory budget.

Native Windows tests cover a 10,000-row, 10-MiB fixture: disk sorting hits the temporary-journal cap, and a TEMP table with journaling disabled hits the temporary-database cap. The unchanged rows and original body remain readable afterward. A diagnostic overlay removing only the temporary-database callback branch reproduces that missing cap without reverting the worktree (`temp-vfs-before-db-boundary-tests.log`: TEMP-table creation incorrectly succeeds). The earlier sort-only fixture demonstrated that SQLite sorts use TEMP_JOURNAL rather than TEMP_DB; its initial failure is retained in `temp-vfs-before-tests.log`/`temp-vfs-first-tests.log`, not treated as proof of an uncapped production sorter. A separate integration fixture tightens only its temporary allowance before temporary files open and proves both independently checked-out pooled traffic connections reject oversized TEMP writes while preserving the captured body.

An actual native reader acquired during rollback-mode recovery prevents the final WAL restoration with SQLITE_BUSY. The new regression proves capture and zero-estimate import admission then pause, saved evidence remains exportable, and reader release followed by clear-all restores WAL and subsequent capture. No synthetic SQL driver substitutes for these failures.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic ./locale -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed 102 top-level Windows tests (91 traffic, 11 locale), zero failures and one existing transport opt-in skip (`temp-vfs-full-windows-tests.log`, traffic 23.085s). A current-source Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic binary passed 90 tests, zero failures and the same skip (`temp-vfs-full-linux-tests.log`). Focused native tests passed in `temp-vfs-pooled-tests.log`. Bilingual README storage behavior was updated. Full server/frontend, race-detector, other platforms and non-loopback opt-in tests are not covered by these results.

Windows `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./server -run '^Test(FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|TrafficStorage)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` also passed six focused server integration tests with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/configuration, zero failures/skips (`temp-vfs-server-tests.log`, 4.851s), including evidence/API export, archive round-trip/retry and failed PostgreSQL deletion restoring staged traffic/files. `git -c core.safecrlf=false diff --check` passed.

## REC-024: clear-all recovery at the unchanged WAL allowance (previous checkpoint)

DeleteAll now pins its SQLite connection and distinguishes a rolled-back deletion from a durable deletion. Native SQLITE_FULL before commit can retry the atomic purge using a bounded rollback journal; SQLITE_FULL during post-commit compaction retries only reclamation. Active native readers veto the journal-mode transition with SQLITE_BUSY without losing their snapshot. Idle pool handles are closed during recovery to release their WAL locks. Original busy timeout and the pool's default idle allowance are restored afterward. WAL mode is explicitly restored; restoration failure pauses capture/import admission while reads and exports remain available and clear-all can retry recovery. Existing successful-deletion/best-effort-compaction API behavior remains when WAL is healthy; a committed purge is never rerun because compaction failed. Blob-GC errors still propagate.

The native VFS now also bounds main/temporary/subjournals, including writes, truncate growth, size hints and chunk allocation. Their per-file ceiling is twice the greater of the configured main-index allowance and its physical size at startup, plus 1 MiB; arithmetic saturates instead of overflowing. This accommodates original-page rollback and legacy index recovery without changing the WAL cap. It does not bound other SQLite temporary files or aggregate disk allocation.

The preserved baseline `wal-recovery-before-tests.log` shows eight captures deleted at a 64-KiB WAL allowance but zero space reclaimed after SQLITE_FULL during compaction. The unchanged-allowance regression now reclaims space, restores WAL and resumes capture. Additional native tests prove pre-commit SQLITE_FULL leaves eight captures intact before recovery, an active reader blocks recovery, journal exhaustion rolls back changed body bytes, and a committed eight-row purge retains its reader's old rows/body and later reclaims space without deleting those rows again. The journal fixture was corrected from writing identical zero bytes to actually changing body bytes; the original failed test log remains retained.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic ./locale -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed 97 top-level Windows tests (86 traffic, 11 locale), zero failures and one existing transport opt-in skip (`wal-recovery-final-windows-tests.log`, traffic 21.491s). The current implementation's Linux/amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic binary passed 85 tests, zero failures and the same skip (`wal-recovery-final-linux-tests.log`). The subsequently added pre-commit and committed-reader regressions passed separately on both platforms with `-run '^TestMinimumWALPurge'` and `-run '^TestMinimumWALCommitted'` (`wal-recovery-precommit-tests.log`, `wal-recovery-precommit-linux-tests.log`, `wal-recovery-committed-windows-tests.log`, `wal-recovery-committed-linux-tests.log`). Windows `go test ./server -run '^Test(FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|TrafficStorage)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/configuration passed six tests without skips (`wal-recovery-server-tests.log`, 4.804s). English/Korean README and error catalogs were updated. Full server/frontend, race-detector, other platforms and non-loopback opt-in checks were not rerun.

REC-024 remains partial: other temporary files, legacy/crash-retained storage, archive staging and aggregate disk bounds remain open. Checkpoint remains 34 fixed/five partial. No new scanner, paid provider, dependency download, deployment, commit/push or global OS change was used.

## REC-024: WAL write boundary integrated into pooled traffic connections (previous checkpoint)

Traffic.Open now registers the store-specific bounded VFS and binds every connector-created SQLite connection to it. The wrapper preserves the pinned driver's prepare/begin/execute/query/ping/reset/validation/file-control interfaces. Registration references are held before native open and released after native close, including failed opens; Traffic.Close requests cleanup without freeing callbacks still used by checked-out connections. A late connection remains readable and its close releases the registration. Ordinary databases retain the default VFS.

Fresh schema bootstrap occurs in rollback-journal mode under the existing main-index allocation ceiling, then WAL is explicitly enabled and verified before traffic is served. This preserves the supported 65536-byte WAL minimum without allowing unbounded WAL bootstrap. SQLite URI paths now encode reserved characters and use an absolute path derived from the supplied data directory, preserving relative-path capture/restart; Linux additionally tests a literal question mark. Existing oversized main-index evidence remains readable/purgeable as before.

The former maintenance overshoot fixture is now an actual production-boundary regression: eight 200-KiB binary bodies are captured, the store is reopened with a 64-KiB WAL allowance, and DeleteHost returns native SQLITE_FULL before WAL growth crosses that allowance. The transaction rolls back, every saved body matches byte-for-byte, and a pinned native SQLite snapshot remains unchanged. Evidence still exports successfully. Reopening the fixture with sufficient configured capacity permits DeleteAll to recover normally. An operation too large for a small allowance is currently rejected; preserving same-limit maintenance recovery still needs work and is not hidden by this test's larger-capacity retry.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed 83 native Windows tests, zero failures and one existing TestProxyClientTransportAdmission opt-in skip (`wal-vfs-integrated-full-windows-tests.log`, 17.235s). Current-source GOOS=linux/GOARCH=amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic binary passed 82 Linux tests, zero failures and the same skip (`wal-vfs-integrated-full-linux-tests.log`). Focused integration and initial bootstrap/index tests also passed (`wal-vfs-integrated-windows-tests.log`, `wal-vfs-integration-first-windows-tests.log`). Windows `go test ./server -run '^Test(FindingTraffic|ManagerDeleteTaskRestoresFilesAndTraffic|TrafficStorage)' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable config/PostgreSQL passed six tests without skips, covering finding/evidence export, archive round-trip/retry, and PostgreSQL deletion failure restoring staged files/traffic (`wal-vfs-integrated-server-tests.log`, 4.962s). Full server/frontend, race-detector, non-loopback transport opt-in and other platforms were not run for this change.

The new WAL write boundary addresses per-write transaction overshoot, including maintenance, rather than merely estimating capture growth. It does not shrink already oversized/crash-retained files, enforce all temporary/journal/staging/legacy allocation, guarantee aggregate free disk space, or finish small-limit maintenance recovery. REC-024 remains partial and the checkpoint remains 34 fixed/five partial. No paid provider, new scanner, dependency download, deployment, commit/push or global OS change was used.

## REC-024: native WAL write-boundary component implemented, integration pending (historical)

`traffic/wal_vfs.go` adds a store-specific SQLite VFS shim over the pinned modernc native implementation. It delegates ordinary native reads, locks, shared memory and synchronization, and rejects WAL writes/truncate growth beyond the configured byte allowance before delegation with SQLITE_FULL. It also rejects oversized allocation hints and nonzero chunk sizes that could round an otherwise valid write beyond the boundary. Ordinary databases retain the unchanged default VFS. Native callback tables live in libc allocations and are freed on file close; unregistering refuses live file handles and supports retry/idempotent close. This uses the existing dependencies, with ABI adapters grounded in the pinned modernc source and [SQLite's I/O methods](https://www.sqlite.org/c3ref/io_methods.html) and [VFS API](https://www.sqlite.org/c3ref/vfs.html).

The component is deliberately not wired into Traffic.Open yet. A hard write failure on oversized deletion must preserve the application's recovery behavior and atomic staged deletions; production integration also needs connection lifetime ownership across pooling/late close and URI/path handling. Bootstrap must preserve the existing 64-KiB minimum without allowing schema creation to bypass a supposedly active write boundary. Existing/legacy evidence must remain readable and recoverable. No capture, import or maintenance path is claimed fixed merely because this component's isolated native test passes.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic -run '^TestBoundedWALVFS' -count=1 -v -timeout=30s` passed two native Windows tests; the current-source GOOS=linux/GOARCH=amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 traffic binary passed the same tests in the existing container (`wal-vfs-windows-tests.log`, `wal-vfs-linux-tests.log`). A real 256-KiB SQLite insertion against a 64-KiB WAL cap returns SQLITE_FULL before growth crosses the cap. Committed data and a pinned snapshot remain unchanged; a small insertion succeeds after rollback/checkpoint. Separate direct callback checks exercise allocation hints, chunk rounding, truncate growth and offset-overflow rejection. Another real SQLite database using the default VFS still accepts a larger WAL write, and registration cleanup/idempotence are checked. The callbacks were corrected to restore the original native method table before close; the final source was tested on both platforms.

Full native Windows `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./traffic -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed 80 tests with zero failures and one existing opt-in skip, TestProxyClientTransportAdmission (`wal-vfs-compatibility-windows-tests.log`, 13.738s). Full Linux traffic, full application/frontend and race-detector suites were not run for this component. Production wiring is pending, so the previously reproduced DeleteHost overshoot remains open. Checkpoint stays 34 fixed/five partial. No paid provider, scanner, new dependency download, deployment or global OS setting was used.

## REC-024: maintenance WAL overshoot reproduced on Windows/Linux

A current-source diagnostic reproduces a maintenance write beyond the configured WAL allowance: eight real 200-KiB binary traffic captures are persisted, the store is reopened with `SKOPOS_TRAFFIC_WAL_BYTES=65536`, a real SQLite snapshot is pinned, and DeleteHost commits all eight deletions. The live WAL then measures 70072 bytes while the retained snapshot still sees eight rows and the current database sees zero. This is 4536 bytes over the allowance. The snapshot deliberately uses SQLite directly without the archive-files outer lock, so the probe exercises storage behavior instead of serializing the deletion behind the application's export lock. No network target or product traffic beyond the synthetic fixture was used.

The diagnostic ran successfully on both native Windows and the existing Linux container, meaning it reproduced the gap on both platforms, not that the quota passed. Commands: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test -overlay <wal-maintenance-probe-overlay.json> ./traffic -run '^TestWALMaintenanceBoundaryProbe$' -count=1 -v -timeout=45s`; Linux uses the same overlay with GOOS=linux/GOARCH=amd64/CGO_ENABLED=0 to compile the test binary, then runs that exact test in the existing container. Artifacts are `wal-maintenance-probe_test.go`, `wal-maintenance-probe-overlay.json`, `wal-maintenance-probe-windows.log` and `wal-maintenance-probe-linux.log` under the existing local test directory. The overlay adds a diagnostic test without changing repository tests or the acceptance criteria. Full project/frontend suites were not rerun, and application code was not modified in this investigation.

[SQLite documents](https://www.sqlite.org/wal.html) that automatic checkpointing occurs after commit; [journal_size_limit](https://www.sqlite.org/pragma.html#pragma_journal_size_limit) controls retained journals/WAL after relevant commit/reset events, not an independent pre-write ceiling. The pinned modernc.org/sqlite v1.52.0 VFS helper is explicitly read-only (`vfs/patches.go`), so it cannot directly wrap these production writes. A hard boundary must cover capture, import, deletion/maintenance and checkpoint behavior at actual writes while preserving transaction rollback and snapshot semantics. Merely tightening capture estimates does not fix this demonstrated path.

The existing index-budget compatibility test intentionally keeps oversized legacy evidence readable and purgeable. SQLite cannot reduce max_page_count below the current page count; this is a preservation/floor condition to account for, not permission to silently delete evidence or disable recovery. No startup rejection or limit reduction was introduced. REC-024 remains partial, checkpoint 34 fixed/five partial. No new scanner, paid provider, install, deployment or global OS setting was used.

## REC-034: shell parser failures require human approval

Parser errors now mark the program as uninspectable instead of silently skipping it. This applies to direct/incomplete interactive fragments and nested literal programs from -c, eval, pipelines and heredocs. Existing rule priority and the model-independent human approval/deny-on-timeout path remain intact. An old literal-string shortcut in shellProgramInput no longer skips a genuinely supplied `<dynamic>` program; its original no-invented-command assertion is strengthened, while invalid executable syntax now expects approval. Empty input, comments, quoted printable text and known noninteractive noexec programs retain their behavior.

Verification: `go test ./intercept -run '^TestUnparsed' -count=1 -v -timeout=30s` failed before the production fix; data and disabled-policy controls passed. `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/config passed 56 Windows top-level tests. Current-source Linux binaries passed 42 interceptor, three guard and 11 locale tests, zero failures/skips (`parse-review-windows-tests.log`, `parse-review-{intercept,guard,locale}-linux-tests.log`). Actual approval/audit/cancellation fixtures cover 180 cases per platform, using nonexecuting dispatch stubs. The five-minute timer was not waited out. SDK command/script/HTTP compatibility tests also passed, including their 48 bilingual synthetic-provider cases (`parse-review-sdk-windows-tests.log`); full frontend/application suites were not rerun.

This fixes approval for parser failures; it does not reconstruct interactive-session state or enforce an OS sandbox. Deferred code, unsupported execution routes and startup files remain open. REC-034 remains partial, checkpoint 34 fixed/five partial. No paid provider, new scanner, install, deployment or global OS changes were used.

## REC-034: eval/source execution is reviewed

Literal `eval` arguments are joined with spaces and inspected through the existing iterative shell analysis; quoted/escaped destructive heads and request methods cannot remain hidden inside them. Unresolved expansion, globbing or a generated program over the one-MiB analysis limit requires human approval. `source`/`.` with a filename likewise requires approval without reading mutable files or treating their quoted data arguments as commands. Supported `command` and selected `builtin` forms retain these checks; ordinary builtin data/no-execution forms are not projected as external commands. Empty eval/source calls and invalid eval option invocations do not acquire invented executable subjects. Behavior is grounded in the [GNU Bash builtin documentation](https://www.gnu.org/software/bash/manual/html_node/Bourne-Shell-Builtins.html).

Verification: the new `TestLiteralEvalCannotHideRestrictedOperations` and `TestGeneratedEvalAndSourcedFilesRequireHumanApproval` failed against the prior implementation; printed-data/empty-program controls already passed. `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/config passed 54 Windows tests; current-source Linux binaries passed 40 interceptor, three guard and 11 locale tests, zero failures/skips (`eval-review-windows-tests.log`, `eval-review-{intercept,guard,locale}-linux-tests.log`). The guard fixture now exercises 144 bilingual cases per platform with human allow, human deny and context expiry, including generated eval and sourced files; its sink remains a nonexecuting dispatcher stub and the synthetic model reviewer is never used. Actual five-minute expiry was not waited out. A new allow-precedence fixture initially failed because its allow pattern did not match every command; it now matches the exact original input, without changing the production rule ordering.

Ten harmless native Bash probes passed on Windows Git Bash and the existing Linux container (`eval-runtime-probes.py/.json`): joined arguments, --, builtin -- eval, nested eval and invalid eval options. Only printf output was exercised, never a destructive command or network request. An initial probe incorrectly expected a backslash to survive both parsing stages; the fixture was corrected to retain explicit inner quotes. The implementation continued to join actual arguments without changing shell semantics.

SDK compatibility: `go test ./server -run '^TestCustom(OperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK|ConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule)$' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed both tests including 48 bilingual synthetic-provider cases (`eval-review-sdk-windows-tests.log`). Full application/frontend suites were not rerun. No paid provider, new scanner, dependency installation, deployment or global OS change was used.

REC-034 remains partial: unsupported execution wrappers/interpreters, deferred code such as trap/alias functions, implicit startup files, incomplete/unparseable input and runtime/OS containment remain open. Sourced mutable bytes are human-authorized, not attested or sandboxed. The checkpoint stays 34 fixed and five partial families.

## REC-034: opaque shell-program sources require human approval

The shared bash/sh/dash/ksh invocation parser now retains whether input is a command string, executable stdin, a script file, known noninteractive noexec mode, or unresolved options. With interception and a restrictive input rule enabled, unresolved `-c` strings, external script files, unknown stdin and unsupported/dynamic options use human approval independently of the model reviewer. Known noninteractive syntax-check modes and arguments to an inspected literal script retain their roles. Files are not opened during review; approval does not pretend to bind mutable script contents. A variable before a script/options boundary can enable interactive execution, so it cannot silently rely on an earlier `-n`. Source handling follows the [GNU Bash invocation documentation](https://www.gnu.org/software/bash/manual/html_node/Invoking-Bash.html).

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/config passed 51 Windows top-level tests. Current-source Linux binaries passed 37 interceptor, three guard and 11 locale tests. All passed with zero failures/skips (`shell-program-review-final-windows-tests.log`, `shell-program-review-final-{intercept,guard,locale}-linux-tests.log`). The new unresolved-script baseline failed before the implementation. An earlier compatibility test conflated an external script's opaque contents with its quoted data argument; its no-projected-request assertion was preserved and strengthened, while seven explicit script/unresolved-mode cases now require the specific human-approval decision rather than silently passing. Initial failure logs remain available. The guard fixture checks 108 bilingual cases per platform through actual approval/cancellation/audit paths, with a nonexecuting dispatcher stub and an always-allow synthetic reviewer that is never called. The five-minute timer was not waited out.

SDK compatibility: `go test ./server -run '^TestCustom(OperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK|ConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule)$' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed both tests and their 48 bilingual synthetic-provider command/script/HTTP cases (`shell-program-review-sdk-windows-tests.log`). No paid provider, scanner, install, deployment or global OS change was used; full application/frontend tests were not rerun.

REC-034 remains partial: unsupported wrappers/interpreters, eval/source-style execution, implicit shell startup files, incomplete/unparseable fragments and runtime/OS confinement remain unresolved. Human authorization is not sandbox enforcement. The checkpoint stays 34 fixed and five partial families.

## REC-004: unresolved curl/wget request methods require human approval

After normal rule matching, an active restrictive tool-input policy now requires human approval when curl/wget option arguments cannot be resolved, may expand into additional argv elements, or select explicit configuration (`curl -K/--config`, `wget --config/-e/--execute`). The approval uses the existing five-minute denial timeout and never calls the fallback model. Matching operator allow rules retain priority. Known scalar header/body/URL values consumed by data options retain their data role; quoted arrays, unquoted expansions and globs cannot silently supply options. wget's `-H` flag no longer consumes the following method option as though it were curl's header option. Files are not read during matching, avoiding a policy-check/config-use race.

Verification: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./intercept ./guard ./locale -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` with explicit disposable PostgreSQL/config passed 49 top-level Windows tests. Current-source Linux test binaries passed 35 interceptor, three guard and 11 locale tests. All completed with zero test failures/skips. Logs: `method-review-final-windows-tests.log` and `method-review-{intercept,guard,locale}-linux-tests.log` under the existing local test artifact directory. The guard fixture covers 54 cases per platform across both languages, three tools, three unresolved command sources and allow/deny/context-expiry responses, using a nonexecuting dispatch stub. The always-allow synthetic reviewer is never called; original command/text values and audit provenance remain exact after JSONB decoding. The actual five-minute timer was not waited out; timeout fields and context cancellation were checked. The first broader Windows run exposed an invalid byte-comparison assertion against PostgreSQL-normalized JSONB; it was replaced with exact field/value comparisons, and that failed log is retained. An initial Linux invocation had PowerShell argument-tokenization errors; quoted test flags then ran the suites successfully.

SDK compatibility: `go test ./server -run '^TestCustom(OperationsReviewResolvedPlansThroughSDK|ConfiguredCommandCannotBypassExecutorRule)$' -p 1 -count=1 -v -timeout=90s` passed both tests with zero skips (`method-review-sdk-windows-tests.log`), including 48 bilingual synthetic-provider command/script/HTTP review cases. No paid provider, new scanner, install, deployment or global OS change was used. Full application/frontend suites were not rerun for this interception change.

Limitations: implicit `.curlrc`/`.wgetrc`, unsupported wrappers and generated/external programs still require separate controls. This gate is human authorization, not HTTP-method enforcement at an OS/network boundary. REC-004 remains partial; checkpoint remains 34 fixed and five partial families.
