Runtime host forwarding and the standalone injected browser clone reader had independent unbounded text() reads. All four consumers are repaired below.

The Python scripts now use a shared stdlib reader with bounded chunk reads, 8 MiB per-response and 256 MiB aggregate defaults, and 2048 attempted requests. A lock reserves read capacity before network reads so concurrent downloads cannot each spend the same allowance; actual received bytes consume the shared budget even if a response is ultimately rejected. Per-response overflow uses at most one look-ahead byte and closes the response without saving or parsing a partial bundle/page. Response deadlines are checked between chunks, with the opener's socket timeout bounding idle reads. This is not a hard 25-second whole-request deadline.

CLI budget arguments permit larger positive finite limits for legitimate large applications. Existing positional commands remain valid. Static seed/manifest frontiers are capped, chunk expansion and one-time retries remain available within the shared request allowance, and stderr warnings expose omitted responses/inventory as coverage gaps. English/Korean copy instructions include the sibling response_budget.py helper.

Passed: `python -m unittest discover -s skills/api-recon/scripts -p 'test_response_budget.py' -v` (four tests, 1.057s). Tests cover exact per-response boundaries, one-byte overflow, bounded read sizes, 24 concurrent consumers sharing an aggregate budget, 100 attempted jobs constrained by request/byte limits, a continuously available slow body hitting the elapsed-time check, and both actual CLI scripts against local HTTP fixtures. Valid forms, routes, API extraction and manifest chunks survive; oversized pages/bundles are omitted and not saved as usable results.

Passed: `python skills/api-recon/scripts/test_origin_policy.py` (three actual crawler origin/redirect/cookie regressions, 2.022s).

Runtime host forwarding now consumes the decoded fetch stream through bounded reads instead of response.text(). Default limits are 8 MiB per response, 64 MiB shared captured response bytes, 2048 forwarded responses, eight concurrent readers and a 20-second AbortController deadline spanning request headers, redirect processing and body reads. Positive safe-integer settings are configurable through responseBudget. Budget checks occur after each awaited read, so concurrent completions cannot spend stale shared capacity. Response overflow cancels the stream, records a truncated sample and explicit captureError, and returns 502 without replaying a POST. A stalled response is aborted and marked as a deadline failure. No partial body is passed to JSON neutralization. Requests that cannot acquire a capture slot remain browser-owned and receive their original scoped headers. Count limits also bound recorded calls, WebSocket frames/identities, SSE/chunk inventories and route iteration. The artifact includes budget counters and omissions rather than implying complete capture.

Passed: `node --test skills/api-recon/scripts/response_budget.test.cjs skills/api-recon/scripts/credential_policy.test.cjs` (eight tests, zero skips, 2.204s), with NODE_PATH selecting isolated Puppeteer and RECON_CHROMIUM selecting installed Google Chrome. Tests cover exact byte boundaries, truncated prefixes, shared concurrent budgets/counts/slots, invalid settings, cancellation of an infinite reader and actual gzip expansion beyond its wire length. Actual runtime CLI/Chromium fixtures verify an oversized POST receives 502 with exactly one backend POST, a never-finished body receives a deadline 502, normal JSON still changes code 401 to code 0, and both failures appear in the report. Existing actual-browser cookie/storage/origin/redirect/CORS regressions still pass. The first concurrent test exposed stale pre-await allowance (128 captured bytes under a 96-byte limit); the calculation moved after the awaited read, retaining the original assertion. Node syntax checks and git diff --check passed.

The standalone browser preload now inspects a fetch clone through bounded decoded reads with per-document response/count/concurrency/time budgets. Aggregate inspected bytes and retained metadata are each limited to 64 MiB by default. Oversize, timeout, exhausted allowance or SSE returns the original application response and marks the incomplete sample; only the inspection clone is cancelled. Cancellation is deliberately not awaited because a tee branch can wait for the original consumer. Incomplete prefixes never enter JSON rewriting. Native XHR remains application-owned, with additional encoding/JSON work skipped above its inspection allowance. Capture arrays, discovered API/route sets and observation arrays share count/retained-byte limits. Metadata is copied on retention so later mutation cannot expand its recorded memory allowance. Serialization failures omit metadata without breaking the application fetch.

Final verification: `node --test skills/api-recon/scripts/preload_budget.test.cjs skills/api-recon/scripts/response_budget.test.cjs skills/api-recon/scripts/credential_policy.test.cjs` passed all 14 Node tests with zero skips (2.201s), using real headless Chromium for the actual preload and runtime CLI. Five preload scenarios prove normal negative-result rewriting and nested URL discovery, unchanged full large/gzip fetch and XHR application bodies, deadline cancellation without tee deadlock, unchanged SSE streaming, bounded concurrent clones, shared byte/count exhaustion with later requests remaining usable, and copied headers resisting post-capture expansion. Initial fixture fixes normalized CRLF for the explicit configuration anchor and supplied the unauthorized message required by the existing rewriting policy; application assertions were retained.

Final Python regression: `python -m unittest discover -s skills/api-recon/scripts -p 'test_*.py' -v` passed all seven tests (2.888s). Node syntax and git diff --check passed. English/Korean documentation explains finite budget configuration, coverage gaps and original-response behavior. These budgets constrain additional reconnaissance work, not the application's own native browser consumption. No live target, real provider or paid scan worker ran. Backend/frontend suites were not rerun for these script changes.

## REC-030: review the operation that will execute

Confirmed: the SDK reviewed model parameters before custom commands, Python scripts and HTTP requests were resolved. Configured executable content could therefore evade rules inspecting the arguments, and a custom name did not inherit an enabled Bash or WebFetch control. Schema defaults also reached ordinary execution after the earlier review.

All production session factories now bind custom executors to their session's original guard and worker steering hooks. The raw custom-parameter check is deferred until the executable plan is ready; ordinary tools receive their defaults before review. Selected custom names and their underlying Bash/WebFetch executor names share priority-ordered rule evaluation and a single judge decision. Intentionally disabled controls retain their existing behavior.

Commands review their frozen rendered command, parameters, working directory, timeout and session environment before invoking the SDK executor. Python reviews its resolved interpreter, code, planned exclusive temporary file, working directory, stdin, parameter environment and inherited environment digest before writing or spawning. HTTP reviews its actual destination, method, headers, body and resolved proxy before sending; every redirect is reviewed again with its effective method/body and destination. Cancellation or a hook rewriting the resolved operation prevents execution. Explicit administrative custom-tool tests retain their direct execution path.

Tool defaults and configured execution data are copied so mutation during human approval cannot change what runs. Original tool names, exact SDK tool-use IDs and completion status remain in the audit, including deferred calls and multiple approved redirect hops. The classifier follows last-handler registry semantics and SDK-added replacements: a custom name cannot exempt an ordinary replacement tool from its hook. The injected-name list is tied to norma v0.4.3 and must be reviewed on SDK upgrades. If background-manager creation fails for a colliding custom name, both checks are retained rather than dropping its guard.

Verification on explicitly selected disposable PostgreSQL, local fixture endpoints and synthetic providers:

- Passed: 48 real SDK/native-executor cases covering command/Python/HTTP, allow/deny/human approval/human denial, English/Korean and direct-streaming/deferred-nonstreaming calls. Actual allowed effects match the reviewed defaults and frozen plan; denied effects do not occur. Configured Bash rules block hidden command content without a judge.
- Passed: HTTP 307 then 303 redirect chains review each destination and the final GET with an empty body. Denying the final destination prevents its request and completes earlier approval audits with the failed parent result.
- Passed: ordinary default injection is reviewed before execution, hook rewrites and cancellation are rejected, and repeated/deferred calls retain their exact IDs. Last-host-handler and actual SDK WebFetch replacements still invoke the guard; enabled web_search configuration is classified without making a search request.
- Passed: `go test -p 1 ./server ./agent ./intercept -run '^Test(Custom.*Review|CustomConfiguredCommand|ToolDefaults|ResolvedOperation|OperationReview)' -count=1` (5.269s / 1.498s / 0.660s), followed by `go test -p 1 ./agent ./guard ./intercept ./locale -count=1` after the final binding adjustment (9.344s / 0.651s / 1.108s / 0.440s). The final server-focused rerun passed in 5.387s.
- Negative regressions: exact HEAD custom-tool execution writes the denied marker, and exact HEAD session capture reviews `{}` instead of the default. Removing only SDK replacement protection through a build overlay produces zero guard calls and one forbidden loopback WebFetch request. These overlays do not change the checkout.
- Passed: isolated `go test ./db -count=1` (32.968s). An earlier parallel multi-package run shared one fixture database and failed its activity test with a startup-DDL deadlock; isolated execution passed without changing assertions. Subsequent cross-package checks use `-p 1`.
- Passed: `SKOPOS_ADAPTER_E2E=1 go test ./server -run '^TestAgentAdapterE2E$' -count=1 -v` (3.478s), exercising the actual MCP and Pi adapter against the authenticated backend with no configured real provider.
- Full final server check: `go test ./server -count=1 -json` finished in 46.448s with 249 top-level passes, 11 opt-in skips and the same seven baseline Windows failures listed below. No additional failures appeared after the registry fix. Output is retained in `LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/operation-registry-server.jsonl`. The suite is not fully green; the evidence-directory sync and skill-path compatibility failures remain outstanding. `git diff --check` passed.

This fixes review of resolved operations. It does not establish a shell/Python network sandbox or repair REC-022's independent execution-target authorization gap. REC-003's quoted shell-template context remains open. No paid scan worker or real model provider was started.

## REC-032: operator ownership of shared asset read grants

Confirmed: add_task_scope and insert_assets' automatic coverage scope fed the same predicates used by list_assets to return shared credentials. A forged source field in a model tool call could claim approval in its prose, and worker intent asset_ids were read through an unscoped lookup. Company/ICP authorization also followed mutable attribution, allowing agent-created company scope or ICP updates to widen a previously selected company's catalog.

Read predicates now use explicit operator scope and manual asset attachments from the current task and its directly linked source tasks. Agent/automatic entries still drive discovery and coverage, but do not authorize credential-bearing reads. The agent tool cannot select the source of its proposal. Operator approval promotes an existing proposal atomically without adding duplicate coverage rows; later agent rediscovery cannot overwrite an operator grant, reason or manual asset attachment. Worker snapshots use the same scoped lookup.

Company/ICP approval captures asset IDs and the company's approved network identities. Subsequent attribution changes cannot expose an unrelated stored credential, while new discoveries under captured company domains/networks remain available. IP grants use concrete network identity, so a DNS-hosted HTTP service cannot obtain access merely by claiming an approved enrichment IP. Existing live operator company/ICP rows receive one initial snapshot during migration; agent/auto rows are never promoted. Duplicate approval requests do not refresh recorded attribution. Explicit removal/reapproval can authorize a changed catalog.

Archives export the identities referenced by grants, remap granted IDs on restore and drop missing/unparseable grant references instead of authorizing a later asset occupying the old ID. Recorded network rules survive remapping. Legacy archived company/ICP scopes without a snapshot restore as coverage history requiring explicit operator approval; restarting/migrating or agent reproposal cannot approve them. The API exposes operator_authorized, and Overview uses it to offer approval for local proposals and legacy entries. Inherited entries remain read-only. English/Korean descriptions, mock scope promotion and README guidance follow these semantics.

Verification:

- Passed: actual PostgreSQL ID/DSL reads, current/direct-source/indirect-source isolation, automatic/agent coverage preservation, idempotent operator promotion, revocation and manual asset attach/rediscovery/detach. Actual company scope mutation and late ICP attribution do not widen earlier grants; task-creation company selection and newly discovered services on approved networks remain readable.
- Passed: actual Norma SDK tool loops in English/Korean and streaming/nonstreaming modes propose scope with a forged manual source, rediscover an existing credential-bearing service and perform ID/DSL reads without receiving its credential. Coverage and writes remain intact; explicit operator approval makes the credential available. A real worker session omits the stored credential before approval and includes it afterward. Providers are synthetic fixtures; no paid model was contacted.
- Passed: PostgreSQL archive snapshot includes globally stored assets referenced only by the grant. Component restore preserves the recorded authorization after attribution changes; legacy restore remains unapproved through startup migration and agent reproposal, and explicit operator approval enables reads. Numeric-ID remapping drops missing/invalid grant references while preserving network rules and historical finding references. The existing full database archive lifecycle suite also passed.
- Baselines: Go overlays of exact HEAD db/task_assets_context.go reproduce credential disclosure in all four SDK cases; exact HEAD agent/worker.go reproduces the unapproved worker snapshot credential. Both baseline commands fail the corresponding new regressions for the expected leak, without modifying the checkout.
- Passed: go test ./db ./agent ./config ./locale -count=1 against disposable skopos_read_grant_final (44.589s, 50.986s, 1.084s, 1.057s). Passed: go test ./server -run '^TestOperatorScopeApprovalAPI$' -count=1 -v against isolated skopos_read_grant_server (1.657s); unauthenticated approval is rejected, authenticated approval grants reads and deletion revokes them. The engine reports no configured provider and remains idle.
- Passed: npm.cmd test with the isolated Chromium fixture enabled, 27 passed, zero skipped; NEXT_EXPORT=1 npm.cmd run build compiled all 31 routes and TypeScript. A headless Chrome test of the compiled production UI with synthetic API responses covers English/Korean at 1440px/390px, checks local legacy/agent approval controls, inherited/keyword restrictions, exact Bearer-authenticated approval payloads and updated grant labels. This browser fixture does not claim live end-to-end API integration; the separate real API regression covers that boundary.

Logs and browser fixtures: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/read-grant-final-{backend,api,web-tests,web-build}.log, read-grant-baseline/{scope,worker}.log and read-grant-overview-browser.{cjs,log}. gofmt and git diff --check passed. No native scan was restarted. The full server suite was not rerun; its seven previously reproduced Windows baseline failures remain unresolved. Concrete intent execution authorization is separately tracked under open REC-022; this change controls shared asset/credential reads.

## REC-012: database connection diagnostic boundary (partial)

Confirmed: config.Redact returned malformed and keyword DSNs almost verbatim and left URL query values in its display. db.Open wrapped pgx parser/network/server errors, which can include connection input and arbitrary driver text. The local reset helper printed the full DSN as its target and forwarded psql stderr. Exact HEAD baselines reproduce these diagnostic leaks with synthetic credential markers.

config.Redact now displays only a recognized PostgreSQL URL's scheme and network authority, dropping user info, path, query and fragment. Keyword/unparseable inputs use a fixed placeholder. Database open/ping/create failures cross a structural diagnostic boundary: invalid configuration, network failure/timeout, cancellation or server rejection, with a validated SQLSTATE when available. Raw driver errors are not retained as unwrap causes, preventing alternate formatting or localized nested messages from revealing the DSN. English/Korean error templates are registered. Connection input and database creation/initialization behavior are unchanged.

The reset helper uses a fixed credential-hidden DSN label and suppresses psql execution stderr in local and Docker modes, retaining its existing localized actionable failure messages and exit behavior. This covers display, not process inspection: the local psql child still receives the full DSN in argv, and caller-supplied CLI secret flags can also be present in the reset helper's own argv. REC-012 therefore remains partial. No claim is made that environments or arbitrary database/application error messages are secret-free.

Verification:

- Passed: actual pgx malformed URL, invalid TLS-option and keyword-DSN failures through db.Open, in English/Korean, contain no fixture secret markers. Separate structural-category tests cover server messages/details, malformed SQLSTATE, DNS input and unknown errors, retain valid SQLSTATE, and preserve context cancellation classification. config display tests cover user info, password query parameters, keyword/malformed strings and IPv6 destination retention.
- Passed: the actual reset script with an isolated synthetic psql executable, testing success and failure, does not print the supplied DSN or injected driver diagnostic and preserves both outcomes. This fixture does not test child argv secrecy. Passed: actual Docker/PostgreSQL reset integration against explicitly selected skopos_server_security, confirming password reset and old-session revocation without using a project .env or production database.
- Baselines: Go overlays of exact HEAD config/config.go and db/db.go fail the new config/Open leak regressions. The original reset script, executed with the same isolated psql fixture, prints both the password-bearing target DSN and driver diagnostic. No checkout/reset or native scan artifacts were modified.
- Passed: go test ./config ./locale -count=1 (0.559s/0.520s); focused config/database/server diagnostic regressions (config 0.670s, db 0.891s, server 1.995s). Passed: go test ./db -count=1 against freshly initialized disposable skopos_dsn_security (30.791s). Passed: SKOPOS_RESET_E2E=1 go test ./server -run '^Test(AuthResetScriptRevokesSessions|ResetHelperDoesNotEchoDSNOrDriverErrors)$' -count=1 -v (2.705s). gofmt and git diff --check passed. The full server/frontend suites were not rerun; seven known Windows server baseline failures remain open.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/dsn-db-tests.log, dsn-reset-tests.log and dsn-diagnostic-baseline/{baseline.log,reset-baseline.log}. The native [psql connection behavior](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/16/app-psql.html) was checked against the disposable container; putting a full URI in PGDATABASE alone does not reproduce psql's explicit connection-string handling, so that shortcut was not adopted. Twenty-five families remain fixed; 14 remain open, including this partially repaired family. No paid scan/model worker was used.

### Final local reset child-argv repair

Local reset now uses the current existing Skopos backend executable with a one-shot -reset-password flag, before any server/store initialization. The existing pgx driver accepts URI/keyword connection strings; no new connection-string parser, dependency or psql child is introduced. The script passes DSN/field credentials and new password through environment variables; the child receives only -reset-password. It auto-discovers nearby current binaries or accepts --backend PATH. Missing/old binaries fail closed. Local recovery now requires a current backend binary rather than a PostgreSQL client; this changes the standalone script prerequisite and is documented in English/Korean READMEs. Docker/compose recovery keeps its existing psql and named-environment forwarding. Existing CLI/config/environment connection precedence, confirmation and localized script outcomes remain.

The native command validates 1–72 password bytes, generates bcrypt cost 10 using the existing dependency, and executes the same auth.password_hash upsert with a 15-second context. No migration, listener, provider or model session starts. Driver failures use fixed messages without DSN/SQL/driver text. Changing to the same password generates a new salt/hash and revokes the prior password generation. Environment variables are not private against the same OS account or administrators. Operator-supplied --dsn/--db-password/--new-password flags inherently remain in that operator's own argv; use environment/config input or the existing prompt to avoid that caller-side exposure. The recovered finding's child-process propagation is repaired, without claiming those explicit input choices secret.

Passed: `GOTOOLCHAIN=local go test ./cmd/skopos -run '^TestNativeReset' -count=1 -v -timeout 30s` (1.726s), with explicit isolated config and disposable native_reset_20261010 PostgreSQL. Actual URI, keyword and component-field connections write bcrypt hashes that verify against the fixture password. Invalid lengths and credential-bearing malformed connection errors are rejected without secret markers. Passed: `go build -o LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/native-reset-skopos.exe ./cmd/skopos`.

Passed: `go test ./server -run '^Test(ResetHelperDoesNotEchoDSNOrDriverErrors|Auth(Local)?ResetScriptRevokesSessions)$' -count=1 -v -timeout 60s` (4.063s), with explicit disposable skopos_server_security PostgreSQL, SKOPOS_LOCAL_RESET_BACKEND selecting the built binary and SKOPOS_RESET_E2E=1. Actual repository script execution through Git Bash verifies both local-native and Docker/psql password reset, rejection of earlier tokens on their next request, and successful new login. The synthetic child success/failure fixture now models the deliberately changed native backend instead of obsolete local psql: it asserts the exact one-argument child argv, preserved secret DSN in the environment, suppressed injected driver diagnostic and both exit outcomes. This updates the fixture for the implementation change without weakening confidentiality or outcome assertions. The full server suite was not rerun.

Logs: native-reset-command-tests.log and native-reset-script-tests.log under LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/. `git diff --check` passed. Earlier diagnostic repairs and evidence above remain valid; their partial/checkpoint statements describe those earlier states. REC-012 is now fixed: 31 families fixed, 8 open. No additional paid scan worker, provider call, production database or real credential was used.

## REC-036: credential-bearing configuration URLs in diagnostics

Confirmed: provider connection-test logging printed the full BaseURL; malformed proxy errors printed raw configuration both as an explicit argument and inside url.Parse's error. Manager startup and browser-MCP proxy synchronization also printed authenticated proxy addresses. Exact HEAD regressions reproduce all of these sinks.

A shared diagnostic formatter now retains only a supported URL's scheme and host/port, omitting URL user info, path, query and fragment. Invalid/unsupported inputs use fixed placeholders instead of echoing unparseable data. Provider and traffic proxy validation retain their existing actionable localized error templates, but invalid parser causes are replaced with a fixed syntax description and unsupported schemes are not echoed. The manager's successful browser synchronization and startup error log use the same URL boundary. This affects display only: configured provider/proxy values, passwords, transport routing and persisted browser-MCP arguments remain intact. Provider response-body evidence and HTTP status diagnostics retain their existing behavior; this is configuration-URL redaction, not arbitrary response-content filtering.

Verification:

- Passed: URL helper cases cover user/password, path/query/fragment secrets, malformed escapes, unsupported schemes and IPv6 authority handling. Provider tests exercise no-attempt and captured-429 logging, inspect malformed proxy errors independently, and verify that the transport still holds the actual authenticated proxy URL. Traffic validation tests check malformed/missing/unsupported URL errors and retained SOCKS5 credentials.
- Passed: actual NewManager startup against an explicitly selected disposable skopos_diagnostic_security database, with a temporary loopback recording proxy and no external requests/model keys. Valid and malformed global proxies exercise successful browser synchronization plus startup validation failure. Logs contain no fixture credential markers while ProxyAddr and saved browser-MCP arguments preserve the exact configured values. The fixture restores prior settings/MCP configuration and closes its database/traffic storage.
- Baselines: exact HEAD agent/provider.go, traffic/traffic.go and server/manager.go are read through a Go overlay. The provider logging/proxy-error tests, traffic validation test and actual manager startup test fail on those originals. Logs show full user/password/path/query secrets in connection-test logs and malformed parser causes, plus both successful browser-proxy logs and startup error diagnostics.
- Passed: go test ./agent ./traffic ./locale ./diagnostic -count=1 on the final implementation (8.387s, 4.132s, 0.235s and 0.171s respectively), using the isolated agent database. Passed: go test ./server -run '^Test(ManagerProxyDiagnosticsPreserveConfigurationWithoutLeaking|OperationsMCPValidationLanguages)$' -count=1 -v against the disposable diagnostic database (1.961s). gofmt and git diff --check passed. The complete server/frontend suites were not rerun for this diagnostic-only change; the seven previously verified Windows server baseline failures remain open.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/url-diagnostic-tests.log, url-diagnostic-manager-tests.log and url-diagnostic-baseline/{client-baseline.log,provider-proxy-baseline.log,manager-baseline.log}. Twenty-five families are fixed; 14 remain open. No paid scan or model provider was used.

## REC-007: worker context role separation

Confirmed with actual Worker/SDK requests and PostgreSQL-backed assets: the original worker appended full stored asset JSON, including page titles and URLs, and the model-authored intent payload to its system prompt. The two retained reports disagree about enrichment reachability; this repair removes the shared serialization sink regardless of which producer stored the metadata. No real model injection experiment was performed.

The worker system prompt now retains only trusted operating rules, constraints and a numeric intent reference. A run-local provider wrapper supplies the full intent and target snapshot as user-role context on every worker request. It retains the existing per-run snapshot semantics, preserving target data in long/resumed runs without making the fields system instructions. It does not delete titles, URLs, authored task descriptions or evidence. The existing startup reminder remains first at user level, with copied content so caller-owned history is not mutated; this also preserves target context in final side-question checkpoints. Auxiliary requests without the worker's trusted marker are not modified. Human steering stays in normal later user turns. Guards, asset/scope checks, tool definitions, transcripts, settlement and reporting are unchanged.

Verification:

- Passed: real Worker SDK regressions in English/Korean and streaming/non-streaming modes. Each creates an actual stored HTTP-service asset containing an instruction-like page-title sentinel, invokes an inert tool, resumes its persisted transcript, then resumes with a human steering message. Every inspected model request has intact title, URL and intent text at user level and none in System. The numeric system reference, one inert execution, human steering and captured final checkpoint provenance are retained. No shell, external target or real model is invoked.
- A separate request-rebuild regression covers compacted/resumed-style history, repeated injection without mutating the original message slice, and exclusion from auxiliary requests without a worker marker. Actual SDK transcript resume is exercised above; automatic/noa compaction was not force-triggered in this fixture.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <worker-context-baseline/overlay.json> ./agent -run '^TestWorkerTargetDataNeverEntersSystemPrompt$' -count=1 fails against exact HEAD agent/worker.go, across all four language/streaming cases. The original promotes the target-title sentinel, URL and model-authored task text into System on initial, continued and human-steered requests. The final test, including side-question captures, was also run against this baseline.
- Passed: go test ./agent ./locale -count=1 on the final implementation (agent 8.162s, locale 0.263s), using the isolated PostgreSQL test database. Adjacent real guard review-context, side-question, worker-message marker and session-ID tests pass. gofmt and git diff --check passed. Full server/frontend suites were not rerun for this agent-local change; the seven known Windows server baseline failures remain open.

This removes data-to-system promotion, not all model susceptibility to malicious text. Guard enforcement and target authorization remain necessary and are tracked separately. Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/worker-context-tests.log, worker-context-focused-tests.log and worker-context-baseline/baseline.log. Twenty-four families are fixed; 15 remain open. No paid scan or model provider was used.

## REC-014: MCP allocation budgets and setup lifetime

Confirmed: original JSON decoding accepted oversized bodies, Streamable SSE could grow a multiline event or consume unlimited ignored events, and legacy readers allocated full endpoint/response lines. The original legacy constructor detached the GET stream from caller cancellation before a closeable Client was returned.

JSON response reads now have an 8 MiB budget plus one overflow-detection byte; oversized responses fail before decoding. Streamable SSE enforces an 8 MiB total wire-read budget and bounded event assembly, including streams containing many ignored events. A matching response within the budget remains readable without waiting for the long-lived stream to finish. Legacy endpoint setup has a 64 KiB aggregate budget, including comments, and each response line has an 8 MiB allocation budget using fixed-buffer ReadSlice fragments. No partial evidence is returned as a successful response. A failed legacy reader cancels/closes its stream and retains the terminal failure so future calls cannot post requests to a dead response channel.

Legacy setup has a maximum 120-second context covering headers, endpoint announcement and initialization, shortened by any caller deadline/cancellation. A context cancellation callback closes the independent stream during setup. The callback is stopped atomically before returning a successful Client, so cancellation of a short-lived discovery context does not kill an established connection. Close and remote termination still release the stream. English/Korean README instructions document these budgets and lifetime behavior.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./mcphttp -count=1 -v, all 11 top-level tests (0.904s), with no PostgreSQL, model provider or real remote targets. Generated fixtures verify exact-limit JSON success, oversized rejection, bounded wire reads/closure, oversized multiline SSE and many ignored events, cumulative endpoint comments, huge endpoint lines, and huge legacy response lines. A failed legacy stream cannot be reused for another POST. Within-budget multiline responses remain intact.
- Live loopback cancellation fixtures stall before headers and after headers but before an endpoint; both constructors return context.Canceled promptly after caller cancellation. The existing full legacy discovery/call test now cancels its original setup context immediately after construction, then successfully lists/invokes tools with a fresh context. All earlier same-origin destination/redirect/legacy relative-path tests pass too.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <mcp-limits-baseline/overlay.json> ./mcphttp -run '^Test(StreamableJSONResponseBudget|StreamableSSEAggregateBudget|LegacyEndpointAndResponseLineBudgets|LegacyResponseLineBudget|LegacySetupFollowsCallerCancellation|LegacySSEClientDiscoverAndCall|SSEWithinBudgetStillReturnsMatchingMultilineResponse)$' -count=1 fails against exact HEAD mcphttp/client.go. The original accepts oversized JSON, both oversized SSE forms and both oversized endpoint forms; its legacy reader consumes 9,437,227 bytes and delivers the oversized response without closing/cancelling the stream. Both stalled setup cases ignore cancellation until the test releases their listeners. Positive discovery and within-budget parsing remain compatible.
- Passed: go test ./server -run '^TestOperationsMCPValidationLanguages$' -count=1 (1.332s), compiling production callers and retaining bilingual validation against the disposable database. gofmt and git diff --check passed. The complete server suite was not rerun for this transport-local change; its seven previously verified Windows baseline failures remain open.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/mcp-limits-tests.log and mcp-limits-baseline/baseline.log. Twenty-three families are fixed; 16 remain open. No paid model or security scan worker ran.

## REC-009: remote MCP destination ownership

Confirmed against live loopback fixtures: original Streamable HTTP POST redirects, legacy SSE GET/POST redirects and legacy message-endpoint announcements each dispatched requests outside the configured origin. The original client also followed an HTTPS-to-HTTP redirect. Server-selected destinations could receive configured credentials and JSON-RPC requests before any response handling.

Both constructors now validate HTTP(S) destination URLs and install the same redirect policy on the shared HTTP client. Every redirected request must retain the configured scheme, hostname and effective port; checks happen before dispatch, including for session deletion. Embedded URL credentials are rejected; configured Authorization/custom headers remain supported. Host comparison normalizes case and equivalent IP text, and omitted standard ports equal their explicit forms. The existing ten-redirect limit remains. This constrains server-driven destinations, not DNS resolution or an operating-system network sandbox; explicitly configured private/local endpoints remain usable.

Legacy SSE announcements are resolved against the final approved stream URL, then checked against its origin before initialization sends a JSON-RPC POST. Relative/absolute same-origin endpoints, session queries and same-origin GET/POST normalization redirects remain available. Same-origin redirects preserve request bodies and configured credentials. TLS certificate verification defaults and its explicit self-signed-server option remain unchanged. English/Korean README guidance documents the destination contract and header-based credentials.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./mcphttp -count=1 -v, all five top-level tests (0.657s). Five live listener attack cases cover Streamable HTTP redirect, legacy GET redirect, legacy POST redirect, foreign endpoint announcement and HTTPS downgrade; the foreign listener receives zero requests. Endpoint cases cover schemes, ports, protocol-relative URLs, unrelated hosts and injected URL user info. Positive cases exercise actual initialize/notification/tool calls through Streamable redirects, existing legacy discovery/calls and a legacy relative endpoint after GET normalization plus a POST normalization redirect. Default ports and equivalent IPv6 spellings compare correctly.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <mcp-destination-baseline/overlay.json> ./mcphttp -run '^Test(MCPNeverDispatchesOutsideConfiguredOrigin|MCPEndpointOriginBoundary|StreamableSameOriginRedirectPreservesHandshakeAndCall)$' -count=1 fails against exact HEAD mcphttp/client.go. Each live attack case dispatches one request to the foreign listener, and every unapproved announcement variant is accepted. The same-origin Streamable handshake/call case passes unchanged. All target responses, tokens and requests are synthetic loopback fixtures.
- Passed: go test ./server -run '^TestOperationsMCPValidationLanguages$' -count=1 (1.323s), compiling the production callers and retaining bilingual MCP validation behavior against the disposable database. gofmt and git diff --check passed. Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/mcp-destination-tests.log and mcp-destination-baseline/baseline.log. The complete server suite was not rerun for this transport-local change; its previously verified seven Windows baseline failures remain open.

MCP byte budgets and legacy setup cancellation are independent remaining work under REC-014. Twenty-two families are fixed; 17 remain open. No paid model or security scan worker was used.

## REC-026 and REC-035: explicit browser credentials

Confirmed: the backend accepted an ambient artex_token cookie and the frontend placed the full administrator JWT in that host-scoped cookie. Cookies cross ports, even though localStorage and browser origins do not. An actual Chromium fixture sent the original JWT to a same-host foreign-port listener; the original backend accepted a same-site cross-origin cookie-authenticated simple POST and reached the custom-tool test handler.

The backend now accepts only explicit Authorization: Bearer credentials or query tokens used by EventSource. It ignores all cookies, including legacy JWT cookies, on both normal API routes and the independently authenticated password-change route. A stale cookie can no longer override an explicit SSE query token. Login, setup and password refresh retain their existing returned-token contract. This removes cookie-based CSRF authorization without introducing an origin allowlist that would disrupt the existing direct-backend SSE path or API adapters.

The frontend retains the JWT in origin-scoped localStorage and uses only artex_session=1 as a non-secret page-routing hint. The hint cannot grant backend access, and the client guard still requires its local token. Login, setup, password refresh, logout and 401 cleanup synchronize the hint. Next page responses delete incoming legacy JWT cookies before rendering, and client token reads delete them too, covering static exports. Existing localStorage sessions survive migration through the login page; legacy cookie-only API clients must switch to Bearer authorization. Already disclosed credentials cannot be undisclosed by removing a cookie. English/Korean README instructions document the transport change.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./server -run '^Test(CookiesCannotAuthorizeAdministratorOperations|ExplicitSessionTokenCannotBeShadowedByCookie|CredentialBoundSessions.*|WebPasswordChange.*|ConcurrentPasswordChanges.*|AuthResetScriptRevokesSessions)$' -count=1 -v, six tests (3.530s), with SKOPOS_RESET_E2E=1 and the explicitly selected disposable skopos_server_security database. Simple text/plain POSTs to actual custom-tool, LLM-test and password-change routes reject JWT cookies for same-host different-port, unrelated, opaque and absent origins. Forged routing hints cannot authorize reads. Explicit Bearer/query sessions still work, revoke correctly and cannot be shadowed by stale cookies. The actual reset helper is run against the disposable container only.
- The earlier revocation helper now expects every cookie request to return 401, including a newly issued token, while preserving its original Bearer/query success, rejection, unavailable-database and concurrent-reset assertions. This is the intentional removal of the cookie credential protocol, not an assertion weakened to mask a regression.
- Backend baseline overlays only the exact HEAD extractToken function, retaining earlier credential-binding fixes to isolate this defect. go test -overlay <cookie-auth-baseline/overlay.json> ./server -run '^Test(CookiesCannotAuthorizeAdministratorOperations|ExplicitSessionTokenCannotBeShadowedByCookie)$' -count=1 fails: the original cookie-authorized request reaches the custom-tool handler (400 Unknown tool kind instead of auth rejection), and a stale cookie overrides a valid query token.
- Passed: npm.cmd test in web with isolated Puppeteer/explicit headless Chrome, all 27 tests, no skips. New tests check actual auth.ts login/refresh/migration/logout, forged hints, actual NextRequest/NextResponse page redirects and legacy-cookie expiration, and mock routing. A real browser loads the source auth module against two loopback origins, verifies that the foreign-port listener receives no JWT cookie, and verifies that localStorage remains isolated across ports. Original HEAD auth.ts, selected through AUTH_UNDER_TEST, fails the same browser test because the foreign port receives the administrator JWT cookie. These are fixture pages exercising production auth code, not a rendered full-app UI smoke test.
- Passed: NEXT_EXPORT=1 npm.cmd run build in web; TypeScript and all 31 static routes succeed. Passed: SKOPOS_ADAPTER_E2E=1 go test ./server -run '^TestAgentAdapterE2E$' -count=1 -v against fresh skopos_adapter_cookie, all three actual MCP/Pi adapter E2E cases (4.949s), with synthetic evidence and no model keys.
- Full server run: go test ./server -count=1 -json against freshly initialized skopos_cookie_security_fresh, 245 top-level passes, seven failures and nine skips (45.726s). The failures are the same previously verified Windows baseline cases: six finding-traffic/workflow directory-sync Access is denied failures and TestSkillRelPath's slash/backslash mismatch. Exact-database auth fixtures and opt-in adapter/reset tests are verified separately above. No new server failures appeared. git diff --check passed.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/cookie-auth-{backend-tests.log,web-tests.log,web-build.log,adapter-tests.log,server-tests.jsonl} and cookie-auth-baseline/{backend.log,browser.log}. No paid model or scan worker ran. Twenty-one families are fixed; 18 remain open.

## REC-039: ownership of legacy traffic directories

Confirmed with real temporary SQLite/blob storage: an asset-derived host named _blobs selected the shared store as its legacy host tree. Exact-host deletion, archive staging and substring deletion all removed an unrelated exchange's referenced body on original source.

Both host-directory derivation paths now use a shared ownership check. Legacy hosts can own only a single non-reserved child directory of the traffic root. Sanitized underscore-prefixed names are excluded, consistent with existing allHostTrees and legacy blob-reference collection. All-dot/space names are excluded too, including Windows trailing-dot/space aliases. Such hostnames remain valid exact-match SQLite deletion inputs; their rows are removed normally while shared storage is reclaimed only through reference-aware blob garbage collection. Normal legacy directory cleanup, orphan cleanup, transactional rollback, archive journals and current SQLite recording remain available.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./traffic -run '^Test(HostCleanupPreservesSharedBlobStorage|ReservedHostCleanupDoesNotStageStoreDirectories)$' -count=1 (1.235s before the additional Windows alias cases). Fixtures record a large unrelated response into actual content-addressed storage, delete an _blobs row through each of the three paths, and verify the retained blob's exact bytes, exchange reference and remaining row count. Reserved names, slash/backslash aliases and root/parent aliases cannot stage infrastructure directories.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <traffic-cleanup-baseline/overlay.json> ./traffic -run '^TestHostCleanupPreservesSharedBlobStorage$' -count=1 fails against exact HEAD traffic/traffic.go. All three cases lose the unrelated body's file. Baseline execution is restricted to _blobs in test-owned temporary stores, without exercising parent-directory moves.
- Passed: go test ./traffic -count=1 (3.748s on the final implementation, including the additional Windows alias cases), covering current recording, shared/legacy blob garbage collection, exact/substring/purge behavior, rollback/recovery, archives and storage. No PostgreSQL or real assessment target is used. Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/traffic-cleanup-tests.log and traffic-cleanup-baseline/baseline.log. The server's complete task-delete/archive integration suite was not rerun; its previously verified Windows directory-sync baseline failures remain open.

Nineteen families are fixed; 20 remain open. No security scan workers were launched.

## REC-023: bounded provider rate-limit inspection

Confirmed: quotaAwareTransport read an entire remote 429 body into memory before SDK handling, including a second retained copy when raw recording was enabled. It now reads at most 65,537 wire bytes: a 64 KiB inspection budget plus one byte to detect overflow. It closes the original body without draining the remainder. Complete responses up to the budget retain their exact body and existing permanent-quota classification. Successful SSE responses remain unread until the consumer requests data.

An oversized response retains HTTP 429 and normal SDK retry behavior, with a small valid JSON diagnostic replacing the downstream body. An incomplete prefix cannot establish permanent quota exhaustion because unread bytes may identify a transient rate metric. Raw recording preserves the received prefix and original status, adds explicit truncation metadata to Attempts, and labels incomplete RawResponse output. It does not claim an incomplete response is replayable or replace wire evidence with the synthetic diagnostic.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./agent ./llmrec -run 'Test(Provider|QuotaAware|RoundTrip|Capture|NilCapture|IsQuota)' -count=1 (agent 2.949s, llmrec 1.017s). Tests use generated 8 MiB bodies with recording on/off, inspect actual byte-read counts and closure, check 65,535/65,536/65,537-byte boundaries, and verify that successful streams are not prefetched.
- A loopback-only actual Norma/OpenAI SDK fixture sends an oversized 429 followed by successful SSE. The request retries exactly once, succeeds, and retains both captured attempts with the correct truncation flag. Existing explicit-quota tests still stop after one request; ordinary bounded rate-limit bodies remain unchanged.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <provider-limit-baseline/overlay.json> ./agent -run '^TestProvider(429ResponseReadIsBounded|429InspectionBoundary|SuccessfulStreamIsNotPrefetched|RetriesOversized429)$' -count=1 fails against exact HEAD agent/provider.go. Both recording modes read all 8,388,608 bytes; the oversized boundary incorrectly classifies the quota prefix, and the retry recording lacks truncation provenance. The unchanged streaming test passes.
- Passed: go test ./agent -count=1 (7.628s) and go test ./llmrec -count=1 -v (0.588s), using the disposable PostgreSQL test database. No paid provider or security scan was invoked. Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/provider-limit-agent-tests.log, provider-limit-capture-tests.log and provider-limit-baseline/baseline.log.

This closes the 429 inspection finding. General successful-stream recording storage limits and other network clients remain separate open families. Eighteen families are fixed; 21 remain open.

## REC-029 and REC-031: notification transport and severity rendering

Confirmed: the shared notification client's same-host redirect check permitted HTTPS-to-HTTP changes, including a downgrade after an initially HTTP request upgraded to HTTPS. It now rejects any non-HTTPS redirect once the request chain has used HTTPS, before replaying the body or headers. Existing same-host normalization redirects and HTTP-to-HTTPS upgrades remain available; existing host/port checks, local-target policy, retries and delivery limits are unchanged. The new error has English/Korean localization and contains no destination token.

Unknown severity labels previously entered the Markdown body and Feishu lark_md unescaped. Severity now passes through the existing one-line Markdown escaping at all three parsed sinks: shared single/digest entries, Feishu single entries and Feishu digest entries. Plain titles normalize whitespace without adding Markdown backslashes. Raw severity evidence, SeverityLabel, JSON output, rankings, colors and known bilingual labels remain unchanged. Existing HTML/Telegram context escaping remains in place.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./notify -count=1 -json, all 105 top-level tests, no skips (0.879s). Includes existing channel serialization, local HTTP receivers, SMTP fixtures, credential redaction, filtering, localization, packing and retry behavior.
- New client tests exercise actual http.Client redirect handling and doJSON serialization with a synthetic recording RoundTripper, not real TLS endpoints. They verify that HTTPS-to-HTTP and HTTP-to-HTTPS-to-HTTP chains stop before an insecure replay, and that secure same-host 307 redirects retain the body and Authorization header. The existing same-host loopback redirect delivery test also passes.
- New markup tests cover English/Korean single and digest messages, exact escaped link/image/emphasis bytes in shared Markdown and Feishu card elements, retained items and raw evidence, and one-line plain titles. Real messaging-provider parsers were not contacted; tests inspect serialized payload fields.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <notification-baseline/overlay.json> ./notify -run '^Test(NotificationClientRejectsDowngradeBeforeReplay|NotificationClientKeepsSecureSameHostRedirect|SeverityCannotInjectNotificationMarkup)$' -count=1 returns nonzero against exact HEAD notify/http.go, markdown.go and feishu.go. The original client dispatches HTTPS/HTTP chains until its redirect-count cap; the original renderer emits the raw injected image/link and bold text. The secure redirect test remains compatible.
- Passed: go test ./locale -count=1 (0.407s). gofmt and git diff --check passed. Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/notification-remediation-tests.jsonl and notification-baseline/baseline.log. No real recipient or paid scan was used.

## REC-033: log subscriber lifecycle

Confirmed with concurrent execution: the original log sink copied subscriber channels under its mutex, unlocked, and later sent to channels that disconnect cleanup could already have closed. The regression reproduces send-on-closed-channel panics and non-monotonic live sequence delivery on original source.

Publication now sends non-blockingly while holding the same mutex used for subscriber removal and closure. No channel reference escapes for later fanout. Slow/full subscriber buffers still drop live events instead of blocking the engine. Ring history, its bounded capacity, cursor behavior, stderr passthrough and asynchronous database dispatch are preserved. Closure remains idempotent and consumers can drain already buffered entries.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./server -run '^TestLogSink.*$' -count=1 -v (1.586s): four publishers generate 40,000 entries while four subscriber workers perform 20,000 subscribe/disconnect cycles. No panic, stream sequence regression, lost publication count or retained subscription remains. A separate full-buffer test verifies non-blocking publication, the last 64 ring entries and all 256 buffered entries after closure.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <logsink-baseline/overlay.json> ./server -run '^TestLogSinkDisconnectCannotRacePublication$' -count=1 returns nonzero on exact HEAD server/logsink.go. Logs capture four send-on-closed-channel panics, regressing live sequences and a stopped publication count of 4966 instead of 40,000. Panics are caught only in the regression to record test failures; production does not hide them.
- Passed: go test ./server -run '^Test(LogSink.*|UpdateProgressRendersPerSubscriber)$' -count=1 (1.477s), checking adjacent subscriber localization too. No PostgreSQL or live model is used. Race-detector instrumentation was not run; no gcc compiler was available on this Windows host. Full server suite was not repeated for this isolated sink change; the seven verified Windows baseline failures remain open.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/logsink-remediation-tests.log and logsink-baseline/baseline.log. Seventeen families are fixed; 22 remain open. No scan worker was launched.

## REC-005 and REC-013: recon origins and host forwarding

Confirmed with local listeners and the actual runtime script: the original MPA crawler's prefix check followed a different port whose text began with the approved origin, and urllib redirects carried supplied cookies to another origin. The original runtime script attached configured cookies to foreign API requests, initialized localStorage on foreign documents, and followed foreign redirects in Node before returning their bodies under the original approved request URL. An actual Chromium page read the foreign redirect's JSON body despite that destination withholding CORS permission.

The crawler now parses HTTP(S) origin identity (scheme, hostname and effective port) and rejects foreign redirects before sending a request. Embedded URL credentials are rejected. Same-origin links and redirects retain the authenticated crawl and form extraction.

The runtime uses a shared credential_policy.js: configured cookies and localStorage render state are scoped to the exact approved origin. Configured cookies are not placed in the native browser jar; a document-local cookie accessor preserves approved-origin render reads and handles writes to configured names locally. The accessor filters those names from native-cookie reads on foreign documents. All intercepted foreign requests strip authorization headers and explicitly override Cookie with an empty value. This explicit override is necessary: an actual browser test showed Chromium adding a native cookie after an omission-only header filter. The assertion was retained, and the runtime fix stops the leak even after an approved response sets a cookie.

Host-side forwarding, stub responses and response rewriting apply only to approved-origin API requests. Same-origin redirects retain normal POST-to-GET behavior and reapply cookie path checks. Foreign redirects are handed to the browser without a host fetch of the destination, and normal browser CORS applies. Native third-party resources, cross-origin redirects, approved-origin response neutralization and response capture still work. Foreign responses are not captured through Node. This is an origin boundary for this reference harvester, not an operating-system/network sandbox; explicit data exfiltration by the approved page and other tools remain outside this fix.

Verification:

- Passed: python -m unittest test_origin_policy -v in skills/api-recon/scripts/, all three tests (2.163s). Runs the actual crawler CLI against independent loopback listeners; a foreign port literally begins with the approved port text. Foreign links and redirects receive no request, while a same-origin redirect retains its cookie and extracted form.
- Passed: node --test skills/api-recon/scripts/credential_policy.test.cjs, all three tests (2.024s), with RECON_CHROMIUM selecting installed Google Chrome and NODE_PATH selecting isolated test dependencies. Tests cover origin lookalikes, ports, schemes, user info, cookie paths and redirect methods, then launch the actual runtime_harvest.js in headless Chromium against loopback-only fixtures.
- The real browser verifies approved homepage/API cookies, localStorage and rendered cookie reads; foreign API requests and navigation without credentials; foreign document storage absence; retained response neutralization/capture; and CORS rejection of a foreign redirect response that deliberately withholds permission. Original Node forwarding instead returned that fixture body to the approved page with a rewritten success code.
- Baselines run the same tests with SPIDER_UNDER_TEST/RUNTIME_UNDER_TEST pointing at exact HEAD script copies outside the repository. Original Python fails two of three tests and sends the fixture session cookie to /sink. Original runtime fails the browser test: logs show the configured cookie at the foreign API, the storage marker in the foreign document's POST, and the foreign redirect body delivered to the approved page. Source scripts and scan artifacts were not replaced.
- The existing recon lockfile uses remote mirror URLs refused by npm's current remote-package policy, so npm ci could not install it. No policy or lockfile was weakened. The existing declared Puppeteer version, puppeteer-core@23.11.1, was installed from the official npm registry into LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/recon-browser-deps/ for validation only. The initial Edge launch failed; installed Google Chrome completed the actual browser check. No browser profile containing user credentials was used.
- English/Korean recon instructions now require copying credential_policy.js alongside the runtime script and explain the exact-origin boundary. git diff --check passed. Backend/frontend suites were not rerun for this script-only change.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/recon-origin-python-tests.log, recon-origin-node-tests.log and recon-origin-baseline/{python-baseline.log,node-baseline.log}. All credentials, responses and target pages were synthetic local fixtures; no model provider or paid scan worker ran. Fourteen families are fixed; 25 remain open. TLS defaults and response allocation budgets remain independently tracked under REC-020 and REC-015.

## REC-008: credential-bound administrator sessions

Confirmed: middleware and password-change authorization previously checked only the installation signing key and token expiration. Changing the stored password through the web or reset-password.sh left seven-day tokens valid. Sessions now carry a domain-separated HMAC of the stored password generation, without exposing the bcrypt hash. Protected requests compare the signed session against current database state; a read failure returns 503, and missing, legacy or revoked credentials return 401. The credential check requires HS256, expiry and the ARTEX subject. Bearer headers, cookies and SSE query tokens use the same admission path.

Initialization and login issue tokens for the exact hash they initialized or authenticated. A web change authenticates its token against the same hash used for password comparison and conditionally replaces only that hash, so it cannot overwrite a concurrent web change or external reset. The successful response supplies a replacement token, and the existing browser dialog stores it before completing. The mock response also supplies its existing demo token. Other sessions must log in again. The reset helper's existing pgcrypto bcrypt update automatically revokes sessions without restarting the backend, even when resetting to the same password.

Compatibility: existing sessions from versions without this binding require a fresh login after upgrading. Already admitted requests and open streams are not cancelled; subsequent requests and reconnections enforce revocation. Installation signing keys and bootstrap-token generation are unchanged. No production credentials were reset during validation.

Verification:

- Passed: SKOPOS_RESET_E2E=1 go test ./server -run '^Test(CredentialBoundSessionsRevokeOnPasswordChange|AuthResetScriptRevokesSessions|WebPasswordChangeRevokesPreviousSession|ConcurrentPasswordChangesHaveSingleWinner|AuthEnrollmentAndPasswordFlowPG)$' -count=1 -v (3.300s). All five tests ran without skipping on explicitly selected skopos_server_security. Tests cover all three token transports, wrong-password rejection, replacement-token usability, a reconstructed server, unavailable database reads, revoked-token password changes, two concurrent changes with exactly one winner, and a stale update losing to an external reset.
- The reset regression runs the actual repository reset-password.sh through Git Bash and Docker against the disposable PostgreSQL container, with explicit database/user/container arguments. The script is copied into an isolated directory with CRLF normalized to LF; no project .env/config enters the execution. The password is a test-only fixture. A same-password reset rejects the previously accepted token and a new login succeeds.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <auth-session-baseline/overlay.json> ./server -run '^Test(WebPasswordChangeRevokesPreviousSession|AuthResetScriptRevokesSessions)$' -count=1 returns nonzero. The overlay replaces only requireAuth and authChangePassword with their exact HEAD function bodies, retaining new pure helpers for fixture compilation. Both old functions admit a stale token with 204 where the tests require 401. No working-tree source was replaced.
- Passed: npm.cmd test in web/, all 21 tests (0.887s); NEXT_EXPORT=1 npm.cmd run build, production compilation, TypeScript checking and all 31 static routes. Rendered browser checks were not performed.
- Passed: SKOPOS_ADAPTER_E2E=1 go test ./server -run '^TestAgentAdapterE2E$' -count=1 -v (4.365s), all three authenticated MCP/Pi integration cases, using a fresh skopos_adapter_sessions database and synthetic evidence without model providers or target interaction.
- Full server check: go test ./server -count=1 -json on fresh skopos_session_security_fresh returned nonzero with 243 top-level passes, seven failures and six skips (43.430s). The failures are exactly the previously reproduced six Windows directory-sync errors and slash/backslash skill-path error. Database-specific auth tests and opt-in adapter checks skipped there and were separately run above. The independent web-revocation test was added after this full run and passed in the final focused run.
- Existing HTTP fixtures now issue credential-bound test sessions against test credentials; their route and response assertions remain intact. Fixture manager cleanup runs after credential restoration. No authentication bypass was added for tests. gofmt and git diff --check passed.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/auth-revocation-final-tests.log, auth-session-baseline/baseline.log, server-session-remediation-tests.jsonl, frontend-session-tests.log, frontend-session-build.log and adapter-session-e2e.log. Twelve families are fixed; 27 remain open. No paid scan worker was started.

## REC-019, REC-021 and REC-028: concrete target identities

Confirmed against exact original source: raw identity fields unrelated to an asset's persisted type could satisfy its allowlist, persisted IP bound_domains could grant IP authorization, and alternate IPv6 spellings bypassed exact-IP blocks. The matching layer now compares parsed network identities with net.IP.Equal, covering compressed/expanded IPv6, case differences and IPv4-mapped IPv6. Invalid IP text cannot satisfy an exact-IP rule. Match results preserve the original value for diagnostics.

Asset admission now separates broad blocking from concrete authorization. Block rules still inspect supplied and stored domains, root domains, bound domains, IPs and URLs. Allow rules inspect only the concrete identity: the domain for root/subdomain assets, the IP for IP assets, or the URL and its host for HTTP services/endpoints. Unverified bound_domains, HTTP enrichment IPs and irrelevant fields cannot grant permission to a different target. Non-HTTP services advertising both a hostname and an IP must have permission for both directly usable identities. Apps cannot borrow network authorization from fields that their insertion does not persist; disabling the allowlist still preserves app insertion.

The shared typed gate is applied to both insert_assets admission and CheckAssetsIntercept for persisted assets. Rejected insertions do not create assets, derived records or task scope, or increment the mutation counter. HTTP services still retain enrichment IPs and page titles under a domain-only allowlist. Direct IP work remains available through an explicit IP/CIDR allow rule; association metadata remains stored as evidence. This does not claim to constrain arbitrary network operations outside the asset gate; concrete-intent and runtime-target authorization remain open under REC-022 and other families.

Verification:

- Passed: go test ./db ./agent -run '^Test(AssetAuthorizationUsesConcreteIdentity|ExactIPUsesNetworkIdentity|InsertAssetsCannotBorrowUnrelatedAuthorization|InsertAssets.*)$' -count=1 (db 0.910s; agent 2.738s). Includes existing insertion and side-effect behavior, nine exact-IP spelling cases, URL-only IPv6 services, type-specific authorization, and explicit non-HTTP hostname/IP permissions.
- Passed: go test ./agent -count=1 -json, all 75 top-level tests (before adding the independent persisted-IP regression).
- Passed after that addition: go test ./agent -run '^Test(InsertAssetsCannotBorrowUnrelatedAuthorization|PersistedIPAssociationDoesNotAuthorizeTarget)$' -count=1 (1.698s). Actual PostgreSQL insertion and persisted admission reject forged relationships and accept explicit IP authorization.
- Passed: go test ./db -count=1 -json, all 179 top-level tests on the fresh disposable skopos_asset_security_fresh database (32.998s), supplementary tables initialized with existing CI methods.
- The first full DB run on the reused skopos_db_security database failed four tests: three fixed fixture names already existed, and a stale company scope won an attribution lookup. A fresh database resolved all four without changing code or assertions. Both logs are retained.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <asset-identity-baseline/overlay.json> ./db ./agent -run '^Test(ExactIPUsesNetworkIdentity|InsertAssetsCannotBorrowUnrelatedAuthorization|PersistedIPAssociationDoesNotAuthorizeTarget)$' -count=1 returned nonzero in both packages. Seven raw bypasses, the independent persisted relationship bypass and alternate network spellings reproduce on exact HEAD db/asset_intercept_match.go and agent/tools_insert.go. The new unit test that references the added method is excluded from baseline compilation; the old-API regressions above remain intact. The working tree was not replaced.
- gofmt and git diff --check passed. Initial new test compilation used the wrong locale constant spelling and an absent single-ID getter; the fixture now uses locale.En and the existing GetByIDs API. No acceptance assertion was weakened.

Logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/db-asset-authorization-tests.jsonl, db-asset-authorization-fresh-tests.jsonl, agent-asset-authorization-tests.jsonl and asset-identity-baseline/baseline.log. No production data, real target, model provider or paid scan worker was used. Eleven families are now fixed; 28 remain open.

## REC-037 and REC-038: unavailable execution policies

Confirmed with original-source regression tests: a failed tool-registry read returned nine default tools, and database failures permitted execution in all four cold/warm-cache and streaming/non-streaming SDK cases. The tool resolver now returns an explicit non-nil empty catalog when the registry is unavailable. Tool augmentation preserves that distinction, including when finding traffic binding is enabled; the SDK interprets nil as permission to restore its defaults.

Interception rules and selected tools are published only after both reads succeed. Failed reads, malformed selected-tool JSON and invalid enabled regular expressions block execution instead of silently disabling controls. A valid intentionally empty selection still disables interception as configured. Failed loads do not poison the cache, so loading can recover after the database becomes available. The runtime judge reads its settings in one checked query and denies on read failure, including when rule/tool caches were previously populated. Existing deliberate allow/deny/ask behavior is preserved when policy loading succeeds. These controls are not an operating-system sandbox.

Verification used explicitly selected disposable PostgreSQL databases, synthetic providers and inert execution probes:

- Passed: go test ./intercept -count=1 (1.042s), including table-read failure/recovery, malformed JSON and invalid enabled regex checks.
- Passed: go test ./server -run '^Test(ToolRegistryFailureCannotRestoreSDKDefaults|WireTools.*|TaskAgentFactoryGuardsBuiltinWrites)$' -count=1 (2.190s). Actual SDK sessions without guard hooks cannot create the requested temporary file after registry failure, with traffic binding both enabled and disabled.
- Passed before the final invalid-regex refinement: complete intercept and agent suites, 21 and 74 top-level tests respectively; the focused SDK policy-failure and existing approval regressions also passed. The subsequent full intercept run above verifies the final refinement.
- Passed after the final refinement: go test ./agent -run '^Test(PolicyDatabaseFailureBlocksSDKExecution|TaskAgentsEnforceConfiguredApproval|CaptureApprovalLifecycle)$' -count=1 (3.859s). git diff --check passed.
- Original-source comparison: go test -overlay <policy-baseline/overlay.json> on each of ./intercept, ./agent and ./server, restricted to the new failure regressions, returned nonzero in all three packages. Logs confirm policy loading silently disabled controls, four SDK probes each executed once, and the registry returned nine tools. The overlay replaces only server/assembly.go, agent/assembly.go and intercept/intercept.go with exact HEAD contents; it does not change the working tree or create another checkout.

Logs are retained in LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/: intercept-policy-remediation-tests.jsonl, agent-policy-remediation-tests.jsonl and policy-baseline/*-baseline.log. No paid scan, model request or live target interaction was used. At this checkpoint eight families were fixed and 31 remained open, including the partially repaired REC-003. The seven previously reproduced Windows server-suite baseline failures remain unresolved.

## REC-003: custom template interpolation (partial)

Confirmed: renderTemplate repeatedly replaced placeholders in a map iteration, so an inserted parameter value could be interpreted again as template syntax. Quoted parameters containing another parameter name could become double-interpolated shell fragments. Replacement now uses a single pass over the original template with deterministic key order. Placeholder-like text inside parameter values remains literal in command, URL, body and header templates. Existing scalar and JSON parameter rendering remain unchanged.

The new regression fails against original customtool.go and passes after the change. Command placeholders inside existing shell quotation contexts remain a separate open subcase; this family is not counted as fixed. No shell payload or live target was executed in the template tests.

## REC-006: DSL expression boundaries

Confirmed with PostgreSQL: on original code, both OR branches could match assets outside independently appended type, producer-task and declared-scope filters. buildDSLWhereBase now groups the complete compiled expression before callers append their predicates. Parameter numbering, argument binding, DSL semantics, ordering, pagination and the intentional taskID=0 global-query path are unchanged.

Regression fixtures test type-only, producer-task-only, combined type/task, scope membership, combined type/scope, corresponding CountDSL results, and the deliberate unscoped global query. The existing inherited-scope membership test also passes. A build overlay using exact HEAD source reproduces all five query-boundary failures; the same tests pass with the fix. Fixture setup was corrected to use an empty PostgreSQL array instead of NULL for the required task_ids column; no boundary assertion was weakened.

## REC-011: spreadsheet-oriented CSV exports

CSV data cells are neutralized when they begin with formula prefixes, control characters, or whitespace/BOM preceding a formula prefix. Full-width equals/plus/minus/at-sign variants are included. The export prefixes dangerous cells with a tab; encoding/csv places that leading tab inside a quoted field and escapes delimiters and double quotes. Normal text, numeric IDs/counts, localized headers, UTF-8 BOM and export ordering are preserved. Stored findings and the Markdown/JSON evidence remain unchanged.

This follows the Excel-oriented mitigation in [OWASP CSV Injection guidance](https://community.owasp.org/attacks/CSV_Injection). There is no universal mitigation for every spreadsheet and downstream consumer. CSV is explicitly for spreadsheet viewing; consumers requiring exact original values should use JSON. Tests verify CSV bytes and parse round trips; Excel itself was not launched. The earlier apostrophe-only candidate was replaced after checking current guidance on Excel save/reopen behavior.

Tests cover every untrusted summary column, ASCII/full-width prefixes, tab/CR/LF, whitespace/BOM, embedded separators/quotes, English/Korean output, retained numeric fields, unchanged stored content and ordinary bilingual text. The formula regression fails against original findings.go and passes after the change.

Verification for these changes:

- Passed: go test ./db ./report ./server -run '^Test(AssetDSLORCannotEscapeIndependentFilters|QueryDSLInScopeMembership|FindingsCSV.*|ReportLanguagesPreserveUserContent|RenderTemplate.*|ShellQuote)$' -count=1. Final package times: db 1.472s, report 0.648s, server 0.894s.
- Passed: go test ./db ./report -count=1 -json on disposable PostgreSQL, all 177 top-level DB tests and four report tests. The final CSV refinements were then separately verified with go test ./report -count=1 (0.665s); the final expanded DSL fixture was checked in the focused run above.
- Baseline: go test -overlay <expression-baseline/overlay.json> ./db ./report ./server -run '^Test(AssetDSLORCannotEscapeIndependentFilters|FindingsCSV.*|RenderTemplateDoesNotReinterpretParameterValues)$' -count=1 returns nonzero as expected, with failures in all three packages. Original source was loaded through git show; neither the working tree nor original scan artifacts were changed by the baseline check.
- gofmt and git diff --check passed. Database schema supplementary tables were initialized through the existing CI db methods. SKOPOS_CONFIG and SKOPOS_PG_DSN were explicitly overridden for disposable test data. No model provider, target interaction, or scan worker was used.

Full-suite logs: LOCAL_SECURITY_ARTIFACTS/db-report-remediation-tests.jsonl. Original-source comparison: expression-baseline-tests.log in the same directory. All 39 families remain tracked; REC-003 is still open despite the completed interpolation subcase.

## REC-002: planner and task-chat execution controls

Confirmed: both Planner.Plan and MainAgent.Chat configured ModeBypass without Hooks. The production task-agent factory now attaches the task's configured guard to both agents. When a task has no guard object, the factory constructs one from the manager's interceptor. Standalone constructors retain an audit guard; configured interception remains governed by the existing user settings.

Both run paths attach task ID, agent name and an activity emitter before executing tools. Human review requests appear in the corresponding task stream; allow/deny records retain the correct agent attribution. SDK guard hooks coexist with the main-agent side-question capture dependencies and with streaming/non-streaming execution. These hooks enforce configured review controls; they are not a complete operating-system sandbox. Other recovered fail-open and command-resolution families remain separately open.

Regression evidence:

- Sixteen actual SDK-session cases cover planner/mainagent, streaming/non-streaming, automatic allow/deny and human allow/deny. Denied probes execute zero times, allowed probes exactly once, and human requests and persisted approval records carry task/agent metadata. Main-agent side-question snapshots remain present. Probes are inert, and providers are synthetic.
- Four production agentsForTask factory cases exercise the actual built-in Write tool under a deny policy, with a task guard and with the manager-interceptor fallback. No destination file is created; one correctly attributed denial is persisted for each attempt. All destination paths are isolated temporary directories.
- Passed: go test ./agent -run '^Test(TaskAgentsEnforceConfiguredApproval|CaptureApprovalLifecycle|WorkerReviewContextAcrossToolCalls|SideActualChatCheckpointToolResultAndTranscriptIsolation)$' -count=1 -v (3.937 seconds).
- Passed: go test ./agent -count=1 -json, all 73 top-level tests, with PostgreSQL supplementary tables initialized through the same db methods used by CI.
- Passed: go test ./server -run '^Test(TaskAgentFactoryGuardsBuiltinWrites|TaskLLM.*)$' -count=1 -json, all 17 top-level tests.
- gofmt and git diff --check passed. Full server suite and browser checks were not repeated for this backend-only change; the previously reproduced seven Windows baseline failures remain open.

All databases used in these checks are disposable and selected explicitly through SKOPOS_PG_DSN; SKOPOS_CONFIG is overridden.
